Since the Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture is equivalent to its more general weighted versions (by blowing up, see [12, 13]), the stronger implication then follows.
Part I Proof of Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture
4 Proof Modulo Key Lemmas
4.1 More on Farkas’ Lemma for Triangle Decompositions
We now proceed to formalize further the study of the implications of Farkas’ Lemma for $K_{3}$ -decompositions. Let us provide names to these notions. For the purposes of this paper, we make the following definitions.
Definition 4.1.
An edge-weighting $\phi$ of a graph $G$ is a function from $E(G)$ to $\mathbb{R}$ . The value of $\phi$ , denoted $|\phi|$ , is $\sum_{e\in E(G)}\phi(e)$ .
Definition 4.2.
An edge-weighted graph is a pair $(G,\phi)$ where $G$ is a graph and $\phi$ is an edge-weighting of $G$ . We write $E_{\phi}(G)$ for the edges of $G$ . We say an edge $e$ of $(G,\phi)$ is positive if $\phi(e)>0$ and negative if $\phi(e)<0$ . We let $E^{+}_{\phi}(G)$ denote the set of positive edges of $(G,\phi)$ and $E^{-}_{\phi}(G)$ denote the set of negative edges of $(G,\phi)$ . For all these, we drop the $\phi$ in the subscript if it is clear from context.
Definition 4.3.
We say an edge-weighting $\phi$ of a graph $G$ is triangle-covered if $\sum_{e\in E(T)}\phi(e)\geq 0$ for each triangle $T$ of $G$ .
We note that we also naturally extend definitions for edge-weightings to edge-weighted graphs. Thus Farkas’ Lemma for $K_{3}$ -decompositions (Lemma 2.2) may be restated as follows.
Lemma 4.4 (Farkas Lemma for $K_{3}$ -decompositions restated).
A graph $G$ has a fractional $K_{3}$ -decomposition if and only if every triangle-covered edge-weighting of $G$ has nonnegative value.
Hence the Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture (Conjecture 1.3 in this paper, proved by our Theorem 1.4) is equivalent to the following.
Conjecture 4.5 (Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture equivalent form).
If $(G,\phi)$ is a triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with
$\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ , then $|\phi|\geq 0$ .
Thus for our proof of the above, we will assume for a contradiction that there exists a triangle-covered edge-weighting with negative value and then use discharging rules to move charge (while preserving total charge) such that every edge has nonnegative weight, yielding a contradiction.
Our first observation is that we will be able to assume there are no edges of zero weight and all edges have distinct weights as follows.
Definition 4.6.
An edge-weighting $\phi$ of a graph $G$ is nonzero if $\phi(e)\neq 0$ for all $e\in E(G)$ and is untied if $\phi(e)\neq\phi(f)$ for all distinct $e,f\in E(G)$ .
Proposition 4.7.
If $\phi$ is a triangle-covered edge-weighting of a graph $G$ with $e(G)\geq 1$ and $\varepsilon>0$ is real, then there exists a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighting $\phi^{\prime}$ of $G$ such that $\phi(e)\leq\phi^{\prime}(e)$ for all $e\in E(G)$ and $|\phi|\leq|\phi^{\prime}|\leq|\phi|+\varepsilon$ .
Proof.
Let $\varepsilon^{\prime}:=\min\{|\phi(e)-\phi(f)|:e,f\in E(G):\phi(e)\neq\phi(f)\}$ (where we let this be infinite if there do not exist two edges of distinct weight). Let $\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}:=\min\{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}\}\cdot\frac{1}{3\cdot e(G)}$ . Let $m:=e(G)$ and let $e_{1},\ldots,e_{m}$ be an (arbitrary) ordering of the edges of $G$ . For each $i\in[m]$ , we define $\phi^{\prime}(e_{i})$ as $\phi(e_{i})+2i\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}$ unless $\phi(e_{i})=-2i\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}$ in which case we define $\phi^{\prime}(e_{i})$ as $\phi(e_{i})+(2i+1)\cdot\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}$ . Thus $\phi^{\prime}(e)\geq\phi(e)$ for all $e\in E(G)$ and hence $\phi^{\prime}$ is triangle-covered. Now $\phi^{\prime}$ is nonzero and $|\phi^{\prime}|\leq|\phi|+\varepsilon$ by construction. Finally $\phi^{\prime}$ is untied since otherwise there exist $i\neq j$ with $\phi^{\prime}(e_{i})=\phi^{\prime}(e_{j})$ from which it follows that $|\phi(e_{i})-\phi(e_{j})|\geq\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}$ by construction but also that $|\phi(e_{i})-\phi(e_{j})|\leq(2m+1)\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}$ contradicting the definitions of $\varepsilon^{\prime}$ and $\varepsilon^{\prime\prime}$ .
∎
In light of Proposition 4.7 above, the Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture is now equivalent to the following conjecture (since then if there exist a triangle-covered edge-weighting of negative value there would also exist a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighting of negative value).
Conjecture 4.8 (Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture second equivalent form).
If $(G,\phi)$ is a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with
$\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ , then $|\phi|\geq 0$ .
4.2 Discharging and Strict Sponsorship
To state our discharging rules, we require some additional notions as follows.
Definition 4.9.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph. A triangle $T$ of $G$ is obtuse under $\phi$ if $T$ has exactly one positive edge and is acute if $T$ has exactly two positive edges. An obtuse triangle $T$ with positive edges $e_{1},e_{2}$ and negative edge $f$ is sponsored if $\max\{\phi(e_{1}),\phi(e_{2})\}\geq-\phi(f)$ and in such case we say the sponsor of $T$ is the edge $e_{i}$ such that $\phi(e_{i})=\max\{\phi(e_{1}),\phi(e_{2})\}$ (note such $i$ is unique since $\phi$ is untied); we say $T$ is unsponsored if $T$ is not sponsored.
We note that such a $\phi$ may have a triangle with $3$ positive edges but we do not utilize such triangles for discharging and so do not require a name for them. Meanwhile since such a $\phi$ is triangle-covered, we crucially have that there is no triangle with $3$ negative edges.
We next require the following definition.
Definition 4.10.
Let $G$ be a graph and $e$ an edge of $G$ . We say an edge $f$ is triangle-incident to $e$ if $f$ and $e$ are in a triangle together and we let $T_{G}(e)$ denote the set of edges triangle-incident to $e$ . If $\phi$ is an edge-weighting of $G$ , we further denote $\{f\in T_{G}(e):\phi(f)<0\}$ as $T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)$ and $\{f\in T_{G}(e):\phi(f)>0\}$ as $T^{+}_{G,\phi}(e)$ . [We drop the subscripts if $G$ and $\phi$ are clear from context.]
We are now prepared to define our demand function as follows.
Definition 4.11 (Strict Sponsorship).
Let $\phi$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighting of a graph $G$ . The strict sponsorship demand function for $\phi$ is defined as, for a positive edge $e=uv$ and edge $f=uw\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)$ (and letting $e^{\prime}:=uw$ and $T:=uvw$ ):
$\textrm{dem}(e,f):=\begin{cases}|\phi(f)|&\text{if $T$ is an obtuse~triangle}\\
|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{\phi(e)}{\phi(e)+\phi(e^{\prime})}&\text{if $T$ is an unsponsored acute~triangle}\\
|\phi(f)|&\text{if $T$ is a sponsored acute~triangle and e is the sponsor of $T$}\\
0&\text{if $T$ is a sponsored acute~triangle and e is not the sponsor of $T$}\end{cases}$
Define discharging rules.
Discharging Rule (Demand Proportional): Let $G$ be a graph and $\phi$ a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighting of $G$ . If $e$ is a positive edge of $G$ and $f\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)$ , then $e$ sends $f$ the following amount of charge:
$\textrm{ch}(e,f):=\phi(e)\cdot\frac{\textrm{dem}(e,f)}{\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\textrm{dem}(e,f^{\prime})}.$
For a positive edge $e$ , the final charge of $e$ , denoted $\textrm{ch}_{\textrm{F}}(e)$ , is thus:
$\textrm{ch}_{\textrm{F}}(e):=\phi(e)-\sum_{f\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\textrm{ch}(e,f).$
For a negative edge $f$ , the final charge of $f$ , denoted $\textrm{ch}_{\textrm{F}}(f)$ , is thus:
$\textrm{ch}_{\textrm{F}}(f):=\phi(f)+\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}\textrm{ch}(e,f).$
Note then that by definition the sum of charges is preserved, that is $\sum_{e\in E(G)}\textrm{ch}_{F}(e)=|\phi|$ . Furthermore, it follows from the definition that $\textrm{ch}_{F}(e)\geq 0$ for every positive edge $e$ (in fact, it will equal $0$ if $T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)\neq\emptyset$ but remains positive otherwise). Noting these facts, we have that the Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture (specifically in the form of Conjecture 4.8 above) is implied by the following stronger conjecture.
Conjecture 4.12 (implies Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture).
If $(G,\phi)$ is a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with
$\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ and $f$ is a negative edge of $G$ , then $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\geq 0$ .
4.3 The Linear Approximation Lower Bound
Now we proceed with the linear approximation lower bound mentioned in the proof ideas section. To that end, we make the following definitions.
Definition 4.13.
For a positive edge $e$ and $f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)$ , we define the normalized demand of $f^{\prime}$ for $e$ , denoted $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,f^{\prime})$ , as $\frac{\textrm{dem}(e,f^{\prime})}{\phi(e)}$ .
Definition 4.14 (Linear Approximation Lower Bound).
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices. If $e$ is a positive edge of $G$ and $f\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)$ , then we define the following:
$\textrm{ch}^{\prime}(e,f):=\frac{4}{n}\cdot\textrm{dem}(e,f)\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\cdot\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,f^{\prime})\right),$
and for a negative edge $f$ , we define
$\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f):=\phi(f)+\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}\textrm{ch}^{\prime}(e,f).$
A simple calculation yields that this is a lower bound as follows.
Proposition 4.15.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices. If $e$ is a positive edge of $G$ and $f\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)$ , then
$\textrm{ch}(e,f)\geq\textrm{ch}^{\prime}(e,f)$
and hence $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\geq\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)$ .
Proof.
This follows as
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}(e,f)$
$\displaystyle:=\textrm{dem}(e,f)\cdot\frac{\phi(e)}{\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\textrm{dem}(e,f^{\prime})}$
$\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\cdot\textrm{dem}(e,f)\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{2}{n}\cdot\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\frac{\textrm{dem}(e,f^{\prime})}{\phi(e)}}$
$\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\cdot\textrm{dem}(e,f)\cdot\frac{1}{1+\left(\frac{2}{n}\cdot\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,f^{\prime})-1\right)}$
$\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{n}\cdot\textrm{dem}(e,f)\cdot\left(1-\left(\frac{2}{n}\cdot\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,f^{\prime})-1\right)\right)=\textrm{ch}^{\prime}(e,f),$
where for the inequality we used that $\frac{1}{1+x}\geq 1-x$ for all real numbers $x$ .
∎
In light of the proposition above, Conjecture 4.12 is implied by the following stronger conjecture.
Conjecture 4.16 (implies Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture).
If $(G,\phi)$ is a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ and $f$ is a negative edge of $G$ , then $\textrm{ch}_{F}^{\prime}(f)\geq 0$ .
4.4 Cleaning
To simplify the analysis that will come later in studying the value, we first introduce some further notation as follows.
Definition 4.17.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph. For a negative edge $f\in E^{-}_{\phi}(G)$ , a positive edge $e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)$ and a negative edge $f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)$ , we define the normalized demand of $f^{\prime}$ for $e$ with respect to $f$ as
$\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime}):=\textrm{dem}(e,f)\cdot\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,f^{\prime}).$
Definition 4.18.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph. For a negative edge $f=uv\in E^{-}_{\phi}(G)$ , a positive edge $e=u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)$ and a vertex $x\in N(u)\cap N(v)$ such that $T=u^{\prime}v^{\prime}x$ is a triangle of $G$ , we define the normalized demand of $T$ for $e$ with respect to $f$ as
$\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T):=\sum_{f^{\prime}\in E(T):\phi(f^{\prime})<0}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime}).$
We note then that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T)$ thus equals $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,u^{\prime}x)+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,v^{\prime}x)$ if $T$ is obtuse, whereas if $T$ is acute then $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T)$ equals $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})$ where $f^{\prime}$ is the unique negative edge. [If $T$ consists only of positive edges, then $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T)=0$ .] We note the following useful proposition.
Proposition 4.19.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph and $f=uv\in E^{-}_{\phi}(G)$ . If $x\in N(u)\cap N(v)$ such that $T=u^{\prime}v^{\prime}x$ is a triangle of $G$ , then
$\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T)\leq\textrm{dem}(e,f)\leq|\phi(f)|.$
Proof.
First suppose $T$ is acute. Then $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T)=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})$ where $f^{\prime}$ is the unique negative edge of $T$ . By definition $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})=\textrm{dem}(e,f)\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,f^{\prime})$ . yet $\textrm{dem}(e,f)\leq|\phi(f)|$ by definition and $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,f^{\prime})=\frac{\textrm{dem}(e,f^{\prime})}{|\phi(e)|}\leq 1$ by construction. Hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T)\leq\textrm{dem}(e,f)\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,f^{\prime})\leq\textrm{dem}(e,f)\leq|\phi(f)|$ as desired.
So we assume $T$ is obtuse. Then $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T)=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,u^{\prime}x)+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,v^{\prime}x)$ . By definition $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,u^{\prime}x)=\textrm{dem}(e,f)\cdot\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,u^{\prime}x)$ and similarly $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,v^{\prime}x)=\textrm{dem}(e,f)\cdot\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,v^{\prime}x)$ . Yet by definition $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,u^{\prime}x)=\frac{\textrm{dem}(e,u^{\prime}x)}{\phi(e)}\leq\frac{|\phi(u^{\prime}x)|}{\phi(e)}$ and similarly $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,v^{\prime}x)=\frac{\textrm{dem}(e,v^{\prime}x)}{\phi(e)}\leq\frac{|\phi(v^{\prime}x)|}{\phi(e)}$ . Since $T$ is obtuse and $\phi$ is triangle-covered, we find that $\phi(e)\geq|\phi(u^{\prime}x)|+|\phi(v^{\prime}x)|$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T)\leq\textrm{dem}(e,f)\cdot\frac{|\phi(e,u^{\prime}x)|+|\phi(e,v^{\prime}x)|}{\phi(e)}\leq\textrm{dem}(e,f)\leq|\phi(f)|$ as desired.
∎
As mentioned in the proof ideas section, we now proceed to worst-case bound the terms arising from triangles with vertices outside the common neighborhood of the ends of $f$ . To that end, we define the following.
Definition 4.20.
Let $G$ be a graph on $n$ vertices and $\phi$ a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighting of $G$ . For a negative edge $f=uv$ , we define
$\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f):=\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e):~f^{\prime}\setminus e\in N(u)\cap N(v)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime}).$
We note for the reader that using the notation of $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T)$ , the above has an equivalent formulation, namely $\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f):=\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{x\in N(u)\cap N(v):e\cup\{x\}\textrm{is a triangle of G}}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,T).$
Now we can state our worst-case bound as follows.
Lemma 4.21.
If $G$ is a graph on $n$ vertices , $\phi$ is a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighting of $G$ , and $f=uv$ is a negative edge of $G$ , then
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)$
$\displaystyle=\phi(f)\left(1-\frac{4}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\right)-\frac{4}{n^{2}}\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}~~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})$
Furthermore if $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ , then
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)$
$\displaystyle\geq\phi(f)\left(1-\frac{3}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\right)-\frac{4}{n^{2}}\cdot\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f).$
Proof.
We calculate that
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)$
$\displaystyle=\phi(f)+\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}\textrm{ch}^{\prime}(e,f)$
$\displaystyle=\phi(f)+\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}\frac{4}{n}\cdot\textrm{dem}(e,f)\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\cdot\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e,f^{\prime})\right)$
$\displaystyle=\phi(f)+\frac{4}{n}\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}\textrm{dem}(e,f)-\frac{4}{n^{2}}\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime}).$
Now we note that $\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}\textrm{dem}(e,f)=|N(u)\cap N(v)|\cdot(-\phi(f))$ by the definition of the demand function (namely $f$ demands $-\phi(f)$ in total from every triangle containing $f$ of which there are $|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ such triangles). Thus we find that
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)$
$\displaystyle=\phi(f)\left(1-\frac{4}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\right)-\frac{4}{n^{2}}\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})$
as desired.
Now we prove the inequality as follows. First we turn to bounding the terms inside the rightmost sums with $f^{\prime}\setminus e\not\in N(u)\cap N(v)$ . Namely for $e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)$ , we find that
$\displaystyle\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e):f^{\prime}\setminus e\not\in N(u)\cap N(v)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})$
$\displaystyle=\sum_{x\not\in N(u)\cap N(v):e\cup\{x\}\textrm{ is a triangle of $G$}}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,e\cup\{x\}).$
$\displaystyle\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot|\{x\not\in N(u)\cap N(v):e\cup\{x\}\textrm{ is a triangle of $G$}\}|$
$\displaystyle\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\max\{|N(u)\setminus N(v)|,|N(v)\setminus N(u)|\}$
$\displaystyle\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{n}{4},$
where for the first inequality we used that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,e\cup{x})\leq|\phi(f)|$
by Proposition 4.19, the second inequality follows as at exactly one of $u$ or $v$ is an end of $e$ , and the third inequality follows since $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3n}{4}$ .
Hence we calculate that
$\displaystyle\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e):~f^{\prime}\setminus e\not\in N(u)\cap N(v)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})\leq\frac{n}{4}\cdot\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}\textrm{dem}(e,f)\leq\frac{n}{4}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\cdot|\phi(f)|,$
where we used that $|\{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)|\leq|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ . Substituting this bound yields that (recalling as $f$ is negative that $|\phi(f)|=-\phi(f)$ )
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)$
$\displaystyle\geq\phi(f)\left(1-\frac{3}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\right)-\frac{4}{n^{2}}\cdot\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f),$
as desired.
∎
In light of the lemma above, Conjecture 4.16 now follows from the following even stronger conjecture.
Conjecture 4.22 (implies Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture).
If $(G,\phi)$ is a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ and $f$ is a negative edge of $G$ , then
$\textrm{ch}_{F}^{\prime\prime}(f)\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{n^{2}}{4}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|-1\right).$
4.5 Value Formulation
Now as to how to prove Conjecture 4.22 above, as mentioned in the proof ideas section, the next key is to formulate the ‘value’ of each pair of vertices in the common neighborhood of the ends of $f$ as follows.
Definition 4.23.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph. Let $f=u_{1}u_{2}$ be a negative edge of $G$ . Then for an edge $g=w_{1}w_{2}\in E(G[N(u)\cap N(v)])$ , we define the value of $g$ to $f$ as
$\textrm{Val}_{f}(g):=\sum_{i,j\in\{1,2\}:\phi(u_{i}w_{j})>0}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(u_{i}w_{j},u_{i}w_{1}w_{2}).$
Note that it follows from the definitions that $\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f)=\sum_{g\in E(G[N(u)\cap N(v)])}\textrm{Val}_{f}(g)$ . To understand how our intermediary theorem about values arises in this context, we define the following auxiliary graph.
Definition 4.24.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph. Let $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . We define the filtered common neighborhood graph of $f$ in $(G,\phi)$ , denoted $\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ , as the graph $H$ with $V(H):=N_{G}(u)\cap N_{G}(v)$ and
$E(H):=E(G[N(u)\cap N(v)])\setminus\bigg(E(G[N^{+}(u)\cap N^{-}(v)])\cup E(G[N^{-}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)])\cup E^{+}(G[N^{+}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)])\bigg).$
The key observation is that this auxiliary graph is $K_{5}$ -free as follows.
Proposition 4.25.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph. If $f=uv$ is a negative edge of $G$ , then $\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ is $K_{5}$ -free.
Proof.
Suppose not and let $S$ be a copy of $K_{5}$ in $H:=\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ . Then $|V(S)\cap N^{+}(u)\cap N^{-}(v)|\leq 1$ by definition and similarly $|V(S)\cap N^{-}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)|\leq 1$ . Since $V(H)=N(u)\cap N(v)$ and $\phi$ is nonzero, it follows that $|V(S)\cap N^{+}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)]|\geq 3$ . But then by definition of $\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ , the edges of $S$ in $G$ are negative in $\phi$ and hence $G[V(S)]$ contains a triangle consisting only of negative edges contradicting that $\phi$ is triangle-covered.
∎
The reason for defining such an auxiliary graph is that the edges deleted from the common neighborhood by the filter graph have value zero as follows.
Proposition 4.26.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph and let $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . If $g\in E(G[N^{+}(u)\cap N^{-}(v)])\cup E(G[N^{-}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)])\cup E^{+}(G[N^{+}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)])$ , then $\textrm{Val}_{f}(g)=0$ .
Proof.
Let $g=wx$ . We claim that for a positive edge $e$ with $|e\cap\{u,v\}|=1$ and $|e\cap\{w,x\}|=1$ , $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,e\cup g)=0$ and hence the proposition follows as desired. To prove this claim, we assume without loss of generality (by symmetry) that $e=uw$ . (Hence $g\in E(G[N^{+}(u)\cap N^{-}(v)])\cup E^{+}(G[N^{+}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)])$ .) First suppose $w\in N^{-}(v)$ and hence $g\in E(G[N^{+}(u)\cap N^{-}(v)])$ . Then the triangle $e\cup g$ consists of only positive edges and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,e\cup g)=0$ as desired. So we assume $w\in N^{+}(v)$ and hence $g\in E^{+}(G[N^{+}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)])$ . Once again the triangle $e\cup g$ consists of only positive edges and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,e\cup g)=0$ as desired.
∎
It is also straightforward to upper bound the value of an edge as follows.
Proposition 4.27.
Let $G$ be a graph, $\phi$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighting of $G$ , and $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . If $g\in\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ , then $\mathrm{Val}_{f}(g)\leq 2\cdot|\phi(f)|$ .
Proof.
Let $g=wx$ . The key observation is that for a positive edge $e$ with $|e\cap\{u,v\}|=1$ and $|e\cap\{w,x\}|=1$ , the sum of the the normalized demands of negative edges $f^{\prime}$ in the triangle $G[V(e)\cup V(g)]$ for $e$ is at most $1$ and hence the corresponding term in $\mathrm{Val}_{f}(g)$ is at most $\textrm{dem}(e,f)$ . But then the sum over $\textrm{dem}(e,f)$ for positive edges in the triangle $G[V(f)\cup\{w\}]$ is at most $|\phi(f)|$ and similarly for $G[V(f)\cup\{x\}]$ . Thus the total sum is at most $2\cdot|\phi(f)|$ .
∎
The facts above and Turán’s theorem would be enough to upper-bound the total value of edges in the common neighborhood (i.e. $\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f)$ ) by $\frac{4}{5}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}$ (as the edges have weight at most $2$ and there would be at most $\frac{4}{5}\cdot\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}}{2}=\frac{2}{5}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}$ edges in a $K_{5}$ -free graph on $|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ vertices). However, in order to prove Conjecture 4.22, we need a better upper bound. This will follow by proving an additional property that there is not too much weight on any triangle motivating the following definition.
Definition 4.28.
A $[0,1]$ -edge-weighting $\varphi$ of a graph $H$ is a function $\varphi:E(H)\rightarrow[0,1]$ . We say $\varphi$ is triangle-thin if for every triangle $T$ of $H$ , we have that $\sum_{e\in E(T)}\varphi(e)\leq 2$ .
We thus call a pair $(H,\varphi)$ a $[0,1]$ -edge-weighted graph if $H$ is graph and $\varphi$ is $[0,1]$ -edge weighting of $H$ . We prove the following in Section 5 using techniques from optimization (specifically this follows under the penumbra of graph Lagrangians as invented by Motzkin and Strauss [49]).
Lemma 4.29.
If $(H,\varphi)$ is a $K_{5}$ -free $[0,1]$ -edge-weighted triangle-thin graph on $n$ vertices, then $|\varphi|\leq\frac{n^{2}}{4}$ .
Then in Section 6, we show the strict sponsorship yields the desired triangle-thinness (appropriately scaled) as follows. But first a definition.
Definition 4.30.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph and $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . If $T=xyz$ is a triangle in $\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ , we define the value of $T$ to $f$ as
$\textrm{Val}_{f}(T):=\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)+\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)+\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz).$
Lemma 4.31.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph and $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . If $T=xyz$ is a triangle in $\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ , then $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4\cdot|\phi(f)|$ .
Now we are prepared to prove Conjecture 4.22 assuming the above two lemmas (Lemmas 4.29 and 4.31).
Proof of Conjecture 4.22 and hence of the Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture.
We let $H:=\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ and define an edge-weighting $\varphi$ on $E(H)$ as $\varphi(g):=\frac{\textrm{Val}_{f}(g)}{2\cdot|\phi(f)|}$ for each $g\in E(H)$ . Then it follows from Proposition 4.27 that $\varphi$ is a $[0,1]$ -edge-weighting of $H$ . Similarly, it follows from Lemma 4.31 that $\varphi$ is triangle-thin. Hence by Lemma 4.29, we have that $|\varphi|\leq\frac{v(H)^{2}}{4}$ . Since $v(H)=|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ , it follows that
$\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f)=\sum_{g\in E(G[N(u)\cap N(v)])}\textrm{Val}_{f}(g)=2\cdot|\phi(f)|\cdot|\varphi|\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}.$
Claim 4.31.1.
$\frac{1}{2}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}\leq\frac{n^{2}}{4}\left(\frac{3}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|-1\right)$
Proof. Let $d:=\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|}{n}$ and note the claimed inequality is equivalent to the following (by dividing by $n^{2}$ and substituting $d$ ):
$\frac{1}{2}\cdot d^{2}\leq\frac{3d-1}{4}.$
Rewriting this is equivalent to
$2d^{2}-3d+1\leq 0.$
Since $2d^{2}-3d+1=(2d-1)(d-1)$ , we find that this inequality holds precisely for all $d\in[\frac{1}{2},1]$ . Since $|N(u)\cap N(v)|\leq 1$ , we have that $d\leq 1$ . Crucially since $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3n}{4}$ , we also have that $|N(u)\cap N(v)|\geq\frac{n}{2}$ and hence $d\geq\frac{1}{2}$ as desired.
$\Diamond$
It now follows from Claim 4.31.1 and our previous bound on $\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f)$ that $\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}(f)\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{n^{2}}{4}\cdot\left(\frac{3}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|-1\right)$ as desired.
∎
4.6 Outline of Remainder of Part I
In the remainder of this part, we provide proofs of the key lemmas. In Section 5, we prove Lemma 4.29. In Section 6, we prove Lemma 4.31.
5 Proof of the Value Lemma
In this section, we prove our value lemma, Lemma 4.29. In order to prove Lemma 4.29, we first prove the following lemma which essentially characterizes the extremal configurations value-wise for graphs with $4$ parts and fixed part sizes (where the vertices in each part are symmetric both in regard to neighborhood and weights to neighbors).
Lemma 5.1.
Let $x_{1}\geq x_{2}\geq x_{3}\geq x_{4}$ be nonnegative reals such that $\sum_{i\in[4]}x_{i}=1$ . For $i<j\in[4]$ , let $a_{ij}\in[0,1]$ be real such that for all $i<j<k\in[4]$ , we have $a_{ij}+a_{ik}+a_{jk}\leq 2$ . Then $\sum_{i<j\in[4]}a_{ij}\cdot x_{i}\cdot x_{j}\leq\max\{x_{1}(x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4}),~(x_{1}+x_{4})(x_{2}+x_{3})\}$ .
Proof.
For $i<j\in[4]$ , let $a_{ij}$ satisfy the assumptions and be chosen so as to maximize $\sum_{i<j\in[4]}a_{ij}\cdot x_{i}\cdot x_{j}$ and subject to this the vector $a:=(a_{12},a_{13},a_{23},a_{14},a_{24},a_{34})$ is lexicographically maximized. Let $\varepsilon:=\min\{a_{ij},~1-a_{ij}:~a_{ij}\in(0,1),~i<j\in[4]\}$ . For $i<j<k\in[4]$ , let $T_{ijk}$ denote the triangle-thin inequality $a_{ij}+a_{ik}+a_{jk}\leq 2$ ; we say $T_{ijk}$ is tight if $a_{ij}+a_{ik}+a_{jk}=2$ . For the various cases throughout the remainder of this proof where we derive a contradiction to the choice of the vector $a$ , we simply provide a contradicting vector $a^{\prime}$ . While we leave the formal verification that $a^{\prime}$ is $[0,1]$ -edge-weighting and triangle-thin to the reader, the former follows as we add or subtract at most $\varepsilon$ and only from $a_{ij}\neq 0$ or $1$ while the latter follows from the former for $T_{ijk}$ that are not tight and for those that are as we ensure their sum is preserved in those cases.
First we claim that $a_{12}=1$ . Suppose not. If neither $T_{123}$ nor $T_{124}$ is tight, then $a^{\prime}:=(a_{12}+\varepsilon,a_{13},a_{23},a_{14},a_{24},a_{34})$ yields a contradiction. If $T_{123}$ is tight and $T_{124}$ is not tight, then $a_{13},a_{23}>0$ and hence $a^{\prime}:=(a_{12}+\varepsilon,a_{13}-\varepsilon,a_{23},a_{14},a_{24},a_{34})$ yields a contradiction. Similarly if $T_{124}$ is tight and $T_{123}$ is not tight, then $a_{14},a_{24}>0$ and hence $(a_{12}+\varepsilon,a_{13},a_{23},a_{14}-\varepsilon,a_{24},a_{34})$ yields a contradiction. So we assume that both $T_{123}$ and $T_{124}$ are tight. Since $a_{12}<1$ this implies that all of $a_{13},a_{14},a_{23},a_{24}>0$ . Next suppose $a_{34}<1$ . Then we let $a^{\prime}:=(a_{12}+\varepsilon,a_{13}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},a_{23}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},a_{14}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},a_{24}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2},a_{34}+\varepsilon)$ which yields a contradiction. So we assume $a_{34}=1$ ; but then $a_{13},a_{24}<1$ . Thus $a^{\prime}:=(a_{12},a_{13}+\varepsilon,a_{23}-\varepsilon,a_{14}-\varepsilon,a_{24}+\varepsilon,a_{34})$ yields a contradiction. This proves the claim.
Next we claim that $a_{13}=1$ . Suppose not. If neither $T_{123}$ nor $T_{134}$ is tight, then $a^{\prime}:=(a_{12},a_{13}+\varepsilon,a_{23},a_{14},a_{24},a_{34})$ yields a contradiction. If $T_{123}$ is tight and $T_{134}$ is not tight, then $a_{23}>0$ and hence $a^{\prime}:=(a_{12},a_{13}+\varepsilon,a_{23}-\varepsilon,a_{14},a_{24},a_{34})$ yields a contradiction. Similarly if $T_{134}$ is tight and $T_{123}$ is not tight, then $a_{14},a_{34}>0$ and hence $(a_{12},a_{13}+\varepsilon,a_{23},a_{14}-\varepsilon,a_{24},a_{34})$ yields a contradiction. So we assume that both $T_{123}$ and $T_{134}$ are tight. Since $a_{13}<1$ this implies that all of $a_{12},a_{14},a_{23},a_{34}>0$ . Note that since $a_{14}>0$ and $a_{12}=1$ , we find that $a_{24}<1$ . But then $a^{\prime}:=(a_{12},a_{13}+\varepsilon,a_{23}-\varepsilon,a_{14}-\varepsilon,a_{24}+\varepsilon,a_{34}+\varepsilon)$ yields a contradiction. This proves the claim.
Since $a_{12}=a_{13}=1$ , we find that $a_{23}=0$ . Now we see that $\sum_{i<j\in[4]}a_{ij}\cdot x_{i}\cdot x_{j}=x_{1}(x_{2}+x_{3})+(a_{14}x_{1}+a_{24}x_{2}+a_{34}x_{3})x_{4}\leq x_{1}(x_{2}+x_{3})+(a_{14}x_{1}+(1-a_{14})x_{2}+(1-a_{14})x_{3})x_{4}=x_{1}(x_{2}+x_{3})+(x_{2}+x_{3})x_{4}+a_{14}(x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3})x_{4}$ .
Now next suppose that $x_{1}\geq x_{2}+x_{3}$ . Then $(x_{2}+x_{3})x_{4}+a_{14}(x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3})x_{4}=(1-a_{14})(x_{2}+x_{3})x_{4}+a_{14}x_{1}x_{4}\leq x_{1}x_{4}$ as desired (since $a_{14}\leq 1$ ). So we assume $x_{1}\leq x_{2}+x_{3}$ . But then $(x_{2}+x_{3})x_{4}+a_{14}(x_{1}-x_{2}-x_{3})x_{4}\leq(x_{2}+x_{3})x_{4}$ as desired.
∎
Next we prove a lemma that upper bounds the value when the graph has $4$ parts (a bound that works for all part sizes) using the previous lemma.
Lemma 5.2.
Let $x_{i}$ for $i\in[4]$ be nonnegative reals such that $\sum_{i\in[4]}x_{i}=1$ . For $i<j\in[4]$ , let $a_{ij}\in[0,1]$ be real such that for all $i<j<k\in[4]$ , we have $a_{ij}+a_{ik}+a_{jk}\leq 2$ . Then $\sum_{i<j\in[4]}a_{ij}\cdot x_{i}\cdot x_{j}\leq\frac{1}{4}$ .
Proof.
By Lemma 5.1, we find that $\sum_{i<j\in[4]}a_{ij}\cdot x_{i}\cdot x_{j}\leq\max\{x_{1}(x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4}),~(x_{1}+x_{4})(x_{2}+x_{3})\}$ . Since $\sum_{i\in[4]}x_{i}=1$ and $x_{i}\geq 0$ for all $i\in[4]$ , it follows that $x_{1}(x_{2}+x_{3}+x_{4})=x_{1}(1-x_{1})\leq\frac{1}{4}$ and similarly that $(x_{1}+x_{4})(x_{2}+x_{3})=(x_{1}+x_{4})(1-(x_{1}+x_{4}))\leq\frac{1}{4}$ . Thus $\sum_{i<j\in[4]}a_{ij}\cdot x_{i}\cdot x_{j}\leq\frac{1}{4}$ as desired.
∎
We now use symmetrization to prove Lemma 4.29 (reducing down to four parts and then using Lemma 5.2 above). But first a definition.
Definition 5.3.
Let $(H,\varphi)$ be an edge-weighted graph. We say $v\in V(H)$ is a weight-clone of a vertex $u\in V(H)$ if $N_{H}(u)=N_{H}(v)$ and for every $x\in N_{H}(u)$ , we have that $\varphi(ux)=\varphi(vx)$ .
Let $\sim$ denote the relation where $u\sim v$ for $u,v\in V(H)$ if and only if $u$ and $v$ are weight-clones. We note that $\sim$ is an equivalence relation on $V(H)$ and call the equivalence classes of $\sim$ the weight-clone equivalence classes of $(H,\varphi)$ .
Proof of Lemma 4.29.
Let $(H,\varphi)$ be a $K_{5}$ -free $[0,1]$ -edge-weighted triangle-thin graph on $n$ vertices such that $|\varphi|$ is maximized and subject to that the number $m$ of equivalence classes of weight-clones is minimized.
First suppose that $m\geq 5$ . Since $H$ is $K_{5}$ -free, there exist two equivalence classes of weight-twins $S_{1},S_{2}$ with $e_{H}(S_{1},S_{2})=0$ . For each $i\in[2]$ , let $v_{i}\in S_{i}$ . For each $i\in[2]$ , we define a new edge-weighted graph $(H_{i},\varphi_{i})$ with $V(H_{i}):=V(H)$ ,
$E(H_{i}):=\{xy\in E(H):x,y\in V(H)\setminus(S_{1}\cup S_{2})\}~\cup~\{xy:x\in S_{1}\cup S_{2},~y\in V(H)\setminus(S_{1}\cup S_{2}),~v_{i}y\in E(H)\},$
and
for an edge $xy\in E(H_{i})$ , we define $\varphi_{i}(xy):=\varphi(xy)$ if $x,y\in V(H)\setminus(S_{1}\cup S_{2})$ and $\varphi_{i}(xy):=\varphi(v_{i}y)$ if $x\in S_{1}\cup S_{2}$ and $y\in V(H)\setminus(S_{1}\cup S_{2})$ . (That is $(H_{i},\varphi_{i})$ is obtained from $(H,\varphi)$ by replacing the vertices of $S_{1}\cup S_{2}$ with weight-clones of $v_{i}$ .)
Note that for each $i\in[2]$ , $H_{i}$ is $K_{5}$ -free and $\phi_{i}$ is a $[0,1]$ -edge-weighting of $H_{i}$ . For each $i\in[2]$ , let $s_{i}:=\sum_{y:~v_{i}y\in E(H)}\varphi(v_{i}y)$ . It follows that $|\varphi_{1}|=|\varphi|+s_{1}\cdot|S_{2}|-s_{2}\cdot|S_{2}|$ and similarly $|\varphi_{2}|=|\varphi|+s_{2}\cdot|S_{1}|-s_{1}\cdot|S_{1}|$ . If $s_{1}>s_{2}$ , then $|\varphi_{1}|>|\varphi|$ and hence $(H_{1},\varphi_{1})$ contradicts the choice of $(H,\varphi)$ . Similarly if $s_{2}>s_{1}$ , then $|\varphi_{2}|>|\varphi|$ and $(H_{2},\varphi_{2})$ contradicts the choice of $(H,\varphi)$ . So we assume $s_{1}=s_{2}$ and hence $|\varphi|=|\varphi_{1}|=|\varphi_{2}|$ . But then $(H_{1},\varphi_{1})$ (and also $(H_{2},\varphi_{2})$ as well) contradicts the choice of $(H,\varphi)$ as $H_{i}$ has strictly fewer weight-clone equivalence classes than $H$ .
So we assume $m\leq 4$ . Let $X_{1},\ldots,X_{m}$ be the weight-clone equivalence classes of $(H,\varphi)$ . For $i\in[m]$ , let $x_{i}:=\frac{|X_{i}|}{n}$ . Thus $\sum_{i\in[m]}x_{i}=1$ . For $i<j\in[4]$ , let $a_{ij}:=\varphi(v_{i}v_{j})$ for some $v_{i}\in X_{i}$ and $v_{j}\in X_{j}$ (note the value is the same no matter the choice of $v_{i}$ and $v_{j}$ as $X_{i}$ and $X_{j}$ are weight-clone equivalence classes). Then
$|\varphi|=\sum_{i<j\in[4]}a_{ij}\cdot|X_{i}|\cdot|X_{j}|=n^{2}\sum_{i<j\in[4]}a_{ij}\cdot x_{i}\cdot x_{j}\leq\frac{n^{2}}{4}$
as desired where the final inequality follows from Lemma 5.2.
∎
Remark 5.4.
The $K_{5}$ -free condition is necessary since if $H$ is a graph, the edge-weighting that assigns $\frac{2}{3}$ to every edge is both a $[0,1]$ -edge-weighting and triangle-thin. Hence if instead the hypothesis is that $H$ is $K_{m}$ -free for some $m>5$ , then taking the Turán graph $T(n,m-1)$ with this edge-weighting shows that one cannot achieve an upper bound better than $t(n,m-1)\cdot\frac{2}{3}\approx(1-\frac{1}{m-1})\frac{n^{2}}{2}\cdot\frac{2}{3}$ which for $m>5$ is $\geq\frac{4}{15}n^{2}>\frac{n^{2}}{4}$ .
6 Proof of the Triangle-Thin Lemma for Strict Sponsorship
In this section, we prove Lemma 4.31. To that end, we provide a restatement of said lemma but with the various cases and subcases enumerated (up to symmetry).
Lemma 6.1 (Restatement of Triangle-Thin Lemma with Cases Enumerated).
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph and $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . If $T=xyz$ is a triangle of $\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ , then $\mathrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4\cdot|\phi(f)|$ if either of the following cases hold:
(1)
$x\in N^{+}(u)\cap N^{-}(v),y\in N^{+}(v)\cap N^{-}(u),z\in N^{+}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)$ and any of the following subcases hold: The negative edges of $T$ are precisely
(a)
$xz,yz$
(b)
$xy,xz$
(c)
$xy$
(d)
$xz$
(e)
$\emptyset$
(2)
$x\in N^{+}(u)\cap N^{-}(v)$ and $y,z\in N^{+}(u)\cap N^{+}(v)$ and any of the following subcases hold:
The negative edges of $T$ are precisely
(a)
$xy,yz$
(b)
$yz$ .
The hardest case by far is (1a). The second hardest case is (2a). The third somewhat involved case is (1b). The remaining cases are fairly straightforward.
In all cases, we assume without loss of generality that $\phi(f)=-1$ (for normalization purposes).
To remain consistent across all the cases, we adopt the following labeling scheme. We label the 10 edges of the $K_{5}$ with vertices $u,v,x,y,z$ as follows:
$g_{1}:=ux,~~g_{2}:=uz,~~g_{3}:=vz,~~g_{4}:=vy,~~g_{5}:=xy$
$g_{6}:=xz,~~g_{7}:=f,~~g_{8}:=yz,~~g_{9}:=vx,~~g_{10}:=uy.$
We now label all triangles as follows:
$T_{1}:=g_{1}g_{2}g_{6},~~T_{2}:=g_{2}g_{3}g_{7},~~T_{3}:=g_{3}g_{4}g_{8},~~T_{4}:=g_{4}g_{5}g_{9},~~T_{5}:=g_{5}g_{1}g_{10}$
$T_{6}:=g_{1}g_{7}g_{9},~~T_{7}:=g_{2}g_{8}g_{10},~~T_{8}:=g_{3}g_{9}g_{6},~~T_{9}:=g_{4}g_{10}g_{7},~~T_{10}:=g_{5}g_{6}g_{8}$
For ease of comprehending each case, we further denote $g_{i}$ as $e_{i}$ in cases where $g_{i}$ is positive and $g_{i}$ as $f_{i}$ in cases where $g_{i}$ is negative. Similarly we will write $T_{i}$ in cases where $T_{i}$ is acute and $T_{i}^{\prime}$ in cases where $T_{i}$ is obtuse.
We note that the triangles that could possibly be tallied in the value sum are those with one vertex in $\{u,v\}$ and two in $\{x,y,z\}$ , which are $T_{1}$ ( $=uxz$ ), $T_{3}$ ( $=vyz$ ), $T_{4}$ ( $=vxy$ ), $T_{5}$ ( $=uxy$ ), $T_{7}$ ( $=uyz$ ), $T_{8}$ ( $=vxz$ ) where as the other triangles $T_{2}$ ( $=uvz$ ), $T_{6}$ ( $=uvx$ ), $T_{9}$ ( $=uvy$ ), $T_{10}$ ( $=xyz$ ) will be not be tallied.
We also note that the sign $g_{2}=f$ is always negative by assumption. In Case (1), we also have that $g_{1}$ , $g_{2}$ , $g_{3}$ , $g_{4}$ are positive and $g_{9}$ , $g_{10}$ are negative (with the signs of $g_{5},g_{6},g_{8}$ varying among the subcases). In Case (2) is the same except that $g_{10}$ is instead positive.
6.1 Proof of Lemma6.1(1)
Recall that in this case the positive edges include $e_{1}$ , $e_{2}$ , $e_{3}$ , $e_{4}$ and the negative edges include $f_{7},f_{9}$ , $f_{10}$ (with the signs of $g_{5},g_{6},g_{8}$ varying among the subcases).
6.1.1 Proof of Lemma6.1(1a)
This subcase assumes the remaining negative edges are $f_{6}$ and $f_{8}$ . We thus have positive edges $e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{5}$ that form a $5$ -cycle $e_{1}e_{2}e_{3}e_{4}e_{5}$ and negative edges $f_{6},f_{7},f_{8},f_{9},f_{10}$ which form a $5$ -cycle $f_{6}f_{8}f_{10}f_{7}f_{9}$ . Similarly, here is a breakdown of the acute/obtuse triangles in this case:
$\textrm{Acute Triangles:}~~T_{1}=e_{1}e_{2}f_{6},~~T_{2}=e_{2}e_{3}f_{7},~~T_{3}=e_{3}e_{4}f_{8},~~T_{4}=e_{4}e_{5}f_{9},~~T_{5}=e_{5}e_{1}f_{10}$
$\textrm{Obtuse Triangles:}~~T_{6}^{\prime}=e_{1}f_{7}f_{9},~~T_{7}^{\prime}=e_{2}f_{8}f_{10},~~T_{8}^{\prime}=e_{3}f_{9}f_{6},~~T_{9}^{\prime}=e_{4}f_{10}f_{7},~~T_{10}^{\prime}=e_{5}f_{6}f_{8}$
For this subcase, we calculate that
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7}^{\prime}).$
Note there are two terms above containing $e_{i}$ for each $i\in[4]$ . Each term is at most $1$ by Proposition 4.19; moreover as $\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)\leq 1$ , it follows from Proposition 4.19 that any term containing $e_{2}$ and any term containing $e_{3}$ sum to at most $1$ . The following claim then follows immediately by inspection from the facts listed above.
Claim 6.1.1.
If any of the following hold:
(i)
$\min\{\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1}),~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})\}=0$ and $\min\{\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3}),~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4})\}=0$ ,
(ii)
$\max\{\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1}),~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})\}=0$ or $\max\{\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3}),~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4})\}=0$ , or
(iii)
$\min\bigg\{\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1}),~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5}),~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3}),~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e)4,T_{4})\bigg\}=0$ , $\min\{\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e)2,T_{1}),\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7}^{\prime})\}=0$ and $\min\{\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3}),~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime})\}=0$ ,
then $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ .
Proof. If (i) holds, then the sum of the terms containing $e_{1}$ and $e_{4}$ is at most $2$ , while as noted before the sum of the terms containing $e_{2}$ and $e_{3}$ is at most $2$ and hence $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired.
If (ii) holds, then once again the sum of the terms containing $e_{1}$ and $e_{4}$ is at most $2$ , while as noted before the sum of the terms containing $e_{2}$ and $e_{3}$ is at most $2$ and hence $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired.
If (iii) holds, then the sum of the terms containing $e_{1}$ and $e_{4}$ is at most $3$ , while using what was noted before the sum of the terms containing $e_{2}$ and $e_{3}$ is at most $1$ and hence $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired.
$\Diamond$
Let $\sigma(i)$ denote the index of the $i$ th heaviest edge among $(e_{j}:j\in[5]))$ so that we have $\phi(e_{\sigma(1)})>\phi(e_{\sigma(2)})>\phi(e_{\sigma(3)})>\phi(e_{\sigma(4)})>\phi(e_{\sigma(5)})$ (this is well-defined as the $i$ th heaviest is unique since $\phi$ is untied). We make the following observation.
Claim 6.1.2.
For $i\in[5]$ if $\sigma(i)<\sigma(i+1)$ (i.e. $e_{i}$ is the heaviest positive edge in $T_{i}$ ) and $\sigma(i)<\max\{\sigma(i-1),\sigma(i+2)\}$ , then $e_{i}$ sponsors $T_{i}$ . Symmetrically for $i\in[5]$ , if $\sigma(i)>\sigma(i-1)$ (i.e. $e_{i}$ is the heaviest positive edge in $T_{i-1}$ ) and $\sigma(i)>\min\{\sigma(i-2),\sigma(i+1)\}$ , then $e_{i}$ sponsors $T_{i-1}$ .
Proof. In what follows we use modular arithmetic and omit the taking of moduli unless needed for clarity. By symmetry, it suffices to prove only the first statement. Note $T_{5+(i-1\mod 5)}^{\prime}$ and $T_{5+(i+2\mod 5)}^{\prime}$ are obtuse. As $\phi$ is triangle-covered, this implies that $\phi(e_{i-1}),\phi(e_{i-2})\geq|\phi(f_{5+i})|$ . Since $\sigma(i)<\max\{\sigma(i-1),\sigma(i+2)\}$ , we have that $\phi(e_{i})>\min\{\phi(e_{i-1}),~\phi(e_{i+2})\}$ and hence $\phi(e_{i})\geq|\phi(f_{5+i})|$ . As $\sigma(i)<\sigma(i+1)$ , we also have that $\phi(e_{i})>\phi(e_{i+1})$ and hence $e_{i}$ is the sponsor of $T_{i}$ as desired.
$\Diamond$
Next we utilize the above observation to prove the following crucial claim.
Claim 6.1.3.
All of the following hold:
(a)
$e_{\sigma(1)}$ sponsors both $T_{\sigma(1)}$ and $T_{\sigma(1)-1\mod 5}$ ,
(b)
$e_{\sigma(2)}$ sponsors at least one of $T_{\sigma(1)}$ and $T_{\sigma(1)-1\mod 5}$ and both unless $e_{\sigma(1)}\cap e_{\sigma(2)}\neq\emptyset$ [ more particularly it would obviously be the sponsor of the one $e_{\sigma(1)}$ is not ]
(c)
if $e_{\sigma(1)}\cap e_{\sigma(2)}\neq\emptyset$ , then $e_{\sigma(3)}$ sponsors all incident triangles in which it is the heaviest positive edge and thus $e_{\sigma(3)}$ sponsors at least one of $T_{\sigma(1)}$ and $T_{\sigma(1)-1\mod 5}$ .
Proof. By Claim 6.1.2, it follows that for $i\in[5]$ , if $\sigma(i)\in\{1,2\}$ , then $e_{j}$ sponsors all incident triangles in which it is the heaviest positive edge (since the associated maximum in the claim is at least $3$ as $\sigma(i)\in\{1,2\}$ ). From this, (a) and (b) follow immediately.
Finally we prove that (c) holds. Suppose without loss of generality that $\sigma(3)=i$ . Since $e_{\sigma(1)}\cap e_{\sigma(2)}\neq\emptyset$ , we find that $\max\{\sigma(i+1),\sigma(i-1)\}\geq 4>3=\sigma(i)$ . If $\sigma(i+1)\geq 4$ (and thus $e_{i}$ is the heaviest positive edge of $T_{i}$ ), then $\max\{\sigma(i-1),\sigma(i+2)\}\geq 4>3=\sigma(i)$ (since $\{\sigma(i+1),\sigma(i-2)\}\neq\{1,2\}$ as $e_{\sigma(1)}\cap e_{\sigma(2)}\neq\emptyset$ by assumption) and hence by Claim 6.1.2, $e_{i}$ is the sponsor of $T_{i}$ . The same holds for $\sigma(i-1)$ and $T_{i-1}$ by symmetry and hence (c) follows as desired.
$\Diamond$
We note for the reader that Claim 6.1.3(a)-(c) imply that at least four of $(T_{i}:i\in[5])$ have sponsors (though we do not require this fact specifically).
Claim 6.1.4.
If $\sigma(1)=5$ or $\sigma(2)=5$ , then $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$
Proof. First suppose $\sigma(1)=5$ (i.e. $e_{5}$ is heaviest). Then by Claim 6.1.3(a), $e_{5}$ sponsors both $T_{5}$ and $T_{4}$ and hence by definition of strict sponsorship we find that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})=0$ and $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4})=0$ . Thus Claim 6.1.1(i) holds as desired.
So we assume $\sigma(2)=5$ (i.e. $e_{5}$ is second heaviest). Once more if $e_{5}$ sponsors both $T_{5}$ and $T_{4}$ , then the result follows. Thus by Claim 6.1.3(b), we have that $\sigma(1)\in\{1,4\}$ . Suppose without loss of generality that $\sigma(1)=1$ . Hence by Claim 6.1.3(a), $e_{1}$ sponsors both $T_{1}$ and $T_{5}$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})=0$ . Similarly, we find by Claim 6.1.3(b) that $e_{5}$ sponsors $T_{4}$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4})=0$ . Then $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired unless $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3}),\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime})>0$ . The latter implies that $e_{2}$ is not the sponsor of $T_{3}$ . Since $e_{\sigma(1)}\cap e_{\sigma(2)}\neq\emptyset$ , this then implies by Claim 6.1.3(c) that $\sigma(3)\neq 2$ . Similarly we find that $e_{4}$ is not the sponsor of $T_{3}$ and hence $\sigma(3)\neq 4$ . Thus $\sigma(3)=3$ . But then by Claim 6.1.3(c), we have that $e_{3}$ sponsors both $T_{3}$ and $T_{2}$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=0$ and Claim 6.1.1(ii) holds for $e_{4}$ as desired.
$\Diamond$
Claim 6.1.5.
If $\sigma(1)\in\{1,4\}$ , then $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ .
Proof. Without loss of generality we assume that $\sigma(1)=1$ . By Claim 6.1.3(a), we find that $e_{1}$ sponsors $T_{1}$ and $T_{5}$ . Hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})=0$ .
First suppose $\sigma(2)=2$ . By Claim 6.1.3(b) $e_{2}$ sponsors $T_{2}$ and hence $\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)=0$ and thus $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3})=0$ and $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime})=0$ . Note here that we have $e_{\sigma(1)}\cap e_{\sigma(2)}\neq\emptyset$ , that is the hypothesis of Claim 6.1.3(c) holds. Further suppose $\sigma(3)=3$ . By Claim 6.1.3(c), we find that $e_{3}$ sponsors $T_{3}$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=0$ and hence Claim 6.1.1(iii) holds as desired. So instead suppose $\sigma(3)=5$ . By Claim 6.1.3(c), we find that $e_{5}$ sponsors $T_{4}$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4})=0$ and Claim 6.1.1(iii) holds as desired. So we assume $\sigma(3)=4$ . By Claim 6.1.3(c), we find that $e_{4}$ sponsors both $T_{4}$ and $T_{3}$ . But then we find that $\phi(e_{1}),\phi(e_{4})>\phi(e_{5})\geq|\phi(f_{6})|+|\phi(f_{8})|$ (where we used that $\phi$ is triangle-covered for the triangle $T_{10}^{\prime}$ ). Hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=\frac{|\phi(f_{6})|}{\phi(e_{1})}+\frac{|\phi(f_{8})|}{\phi(e_{4})}\leq\frac{|\phi(f_{6})|+|\phi(f_{8})|}{\phi(e_{5})}\leq 1$ and hence $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired. Thus we assume $\sigma(2)\neq 2$ .
Next suppose $\sigma(2)=3$ . By Claim 6.1.3(b), $e_{3}$ sponsors $T_{3}$ and $T_{2}$ . As $e_{3}$ sponsors $T_{2}$ , we find that $\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)=0$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})=0$ and $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7}^{\prime})=0$ . As $e_{3}$ sponsors $T_{3}$ , we find that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=0$ . We also have that $\phi(e_{1}),\phi(e_{3})>\phi(e_{5})\geq|\phi(f_{6})|+|\phi(f_{8})|$ (where we used that $\phi$ is triangle-covered for the triangle $T_{10}^{\prime}$ ). Hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3})=\frac{|\phi(f_{6})|}{\phi(e_{1})}+\frac{|\phi(f_{8})|}{\phi(e_{3})}\leq\frac{|\phi(f_{1})|+|\phi(f_{3})|}{\phi(e_{5})}\leq 1$ , and hence $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired. Thus we assume $\sigma(2)\neq 3$ .
So by Claim 6.1.4, we assume $\sigma(2)=4$ . So by Claim 6.1.3(b), $e_{4}$ sponsors $T_{4}$ and $T_{3}$ . Hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3})=0$ . But then we use that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7}^{\prime})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime})\leq\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)\leq 1$ . Meanwhile, we also have that $\phi(e_{1}),\phi(e_{4})>\phi(e_{5})\geq|\phi(f_{6})|+|\phi(f_{8})|$ (where we used that $\phi$ is triangle-covered for the triangle $T_{10}^{\prime}$ ). Hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=\frac{|\phi(f_{6})|}{\phi(e_{1})}+\frac{|\phi(f_{8})|}{\phi(e_{4})}\leq\frac{|\phi(f_{6})|+|\phi(f_{8})|}{\phi(e_{5})}\leq 1$ and hence $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired.
$\Diamond$
Claim 6.1.6.
If $\sigma(1)\in\{2,3\}$ , then $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ .
Proof. Without loss of generality, we assume that $\sigma(1)=2$ . By Claim 6.1.3(a), we find that $e_{2}$ sponsors $T_{2}$ and $T_{1}$ . As $e_{2}$ sponsors $T_{2}$ , we find that $\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)=0$ ; hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3})=0$ and $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime})=0$ . As $e_{2}$ sponsors $T_{1}$ , we find that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1})=0$ .
First suppose $\sigma(2)=3$ . By Claim 6.1.3(b), we find that $e_{3}$ sponsors $T_{3}$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=0$ and hence Claim 6.1.1(i) holds as desired. So we assume $\sigma(2)\neq 3$ .
Next suppose $\sigma(2)=1$ . Note here that $e_{\sigma(1)}\cap e_{\sigma(2)}\neq\emptyset$ and hence the hypothesis of Claim 6.1.3(c) holds. Further suppose $\sigma(3)=5$ . By Claim 6.1.3(c), we find that $e_{5}$ sponsors $T_{4}$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4})=0$ ; thus Claim 6.1.1(i) holds as desired. So instead suppose $\sigma(3)=3$ . By Claim 6.1.3(c), we find that $e_{3}$ sponsors $T_{3}$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=0$ ; thus Claim 6.1.1(i) holds as desired. So we assume $\sigma(3)=4$ . But then we have that $\phi(e_{2}),\phi(e_{4})>\phi(e_{5})\geq|\phi(f_{6})|+|\phi(f_{8})|$ (where we used that $\phi$ is triangle-covered for the triangle $T_{10}^{\prime}$ ). Hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=\frac{|\phi(f_{6})|}{\phi(e_{2})}+\frac{|\phi(f_{8})|}{\phi(e_{4})}\leq\frac{|\phi(f_{6})|+|\phi(f_{8})|}{\phi(e_{5})}\leq 1$ and hence $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired.
So by Claim 6.1.4, we assume $\sigma(2)=4$ . By Claim 6.1.3(b) (as $e_{2}\cap e_{4}=\emptyset$ ), we find that $e_{4}$ sponsors $T_{4}$ and $T_{3}$ . But then we also have that $\phi(e_{2}),\phi(e_{4})>\phi(e_{5})\geq|\phi(f_{6})|+|\phi(f_{8})|$ (where we used that $\phi$ is triangle-covered for the triangle $T_{10}^{\prime}$ ). Hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=\frac{|\phi(f_{6})|}{\phi(e_{2})}+\frac{|\phi(f_{8})|}{\phi(e_{4})}\leq\frac{|\phi(f_{6})|+|\phi(f_{8})|}{\phi(e_{5})}\leq 1$ and hence $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired.
$\Diamond$
Finally, we conclude the proof of this subcase via the claims above. By definition $\sigma(1)\in[5]$ . Thus by Claim 6.1.4 (if $\sigma(1)=5$ ), by Claim 6.1.5 (if $\sigma(1)\in\{1,4\}$ ) and by Claim 6.1.6 (if $\sigma(1)\in\{2,3\}$ ), we find that $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)\leq 4$ as desired.
6.1.2 Proof of Lemma6.1(1b)
This subcase assumes the remaining negative edges are $f_{5}$ and $f_{6}$ . Thus we have positive edges $e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{8}$ and negative edges $f_{5},f_{6},f_{7},f_{9},f_{10}$ . Similarly, here is a breakdown of the acute/obtuse triangles in this case:
$\textrm{All Positive}:~~T_{3}=e_{3}e_{4}e_{8},$
$\textrm{Acute Triangles:}~~T_{1}=e_{1}e_{2}f_{6},~~T_{2}=e_{2}e_{3}f_{7},~~T_{7}=e_{2}e_{8}f_{10},$
$\textrm{Obtuse Triangles:}~~T_{4}^{\prime}=e_{4}f_{5}f_{9},~~T_{5}^{\prime}=f_{5}e_{1}f_{10},~~T_{6}^{\prime}=e_{1}f_{7}f_{9},~~T_{8}^{\prime}=e_{3}f_{9}f_{6},~~T_{9}^{\prime}=e_{4}f_{10}f_{7},~~T_{10}^{\prime}=f_{5}f_{6}e_{8}$
For this subcase, we calculate that
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5}^{\prime})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4}^{\prime}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e_{1},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e_{2},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7}).$
Suppose for a contradiction that $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)=\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)+\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)+\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)>4$ . Since $\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)\leq 1$ , we have that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{i})+\textrm{dem}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime})\leq 1$ for any $i\in\{1,7\}$ . Since the sum of values above is strictly greater than $4$ , it follows that all of $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5}^{\prime})$ , $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4}^{\prime})$ , $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1})$ ,
$\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})$ , $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7})$ are nonzero.
Since $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1}),\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})>0$ , it follows that $T_{1}$ is unsponsored and hence $\phi(e_{1}),\phi(e_{2})<|\phi(f_{6})|$ . Since $\phi$ is triangle-covered (in particular for the triangle $T_{10}^{\prime}$ ), we find that $|\phi(e_{8})\geq|\phi(f_{5})|+|\phi(f_{6})|$ and hence $\phi(e_{8})>\phi(e_{1})$ and $\phi(e_{8})>\phi(e_{2})$ . Since $\phi$ is triangle-covered (in particular for the triangle $T_{5}^{\prime}$ ), we find that $\phi(e_{1})\geq|\phi(f_{5})|+|\phi(f_{10})|$ . Hence $\phi(e_{8})\geq|\phi(f_{10})|$ . It follows that $e_{8}$ is the sponsor of $T_{7}=e_{2}e_{8}f_{10}$ . But then $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7})=0$ , a contradiction.
6.1.3 Proof of Lemma6.1(1c)
This subcase assumes the only remaining negative edge is $f_{5}$ . Thus we have have positive edges $e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{6},e_{8}$ and negative edges $f_{5},f_{7},f_{9},f_{10}$ . Similarly, here is a breakdown of the acute/obtuse triangles in this case:
$\textrm{All Positive}:~~T_{1}=e_{1}e_{2}e_{6},~~T_{3}=e_{3}e_{4}e_{8},~~T_{10}=e_{5}e_{6}e_{8}$
$\textrm{Acute Triangles:}~~T_{2}=e_{2}e_{3}f_{7},~~T_{4}=e_{4}e_{5}f_{9},~~T_{5}=e_{5}e_{1}f_{10},~~T_{7}=e_{2}e_{8}f_{10},~~T_{8}=e_{3}f_{9}e_{6},$
$\textrm{Obtuse Triangles:}~~T_{6}^{\prime}=e_{1}f_{7}f_{9},~~T_{9}^{\prime}=e_{4}f_{10}f_{7}.$
For this subcase, we calculate that
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7}^{\prime}).$
Since $\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)\leq 1$ , we actually find that the sum of the above is at most $3$ .
6.1.4 Proof of Lemma6.1(1d)
This subcase assumes the only remaining negative edge is $f_{6}$ . In this subcase we thus have positive edges $e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{5},e_{8}$ and negative edges $f_{6},f_{7},f_{9},f_{10}$ . Similarly, here is a breakdown of the acute/obtuse triangles in this case:
$\textrm{All Positive:}~~T_{3}=e_{3}e_{4}e_{8},$
$\textrm{Acute Triangles:}~~T_{1}=e_{1}e_{2}f_{6},~~T_{2}=e_{2}e_{3}f_{7},~~T_{4}=e_{4}e_{5}f_{9},~~T_{5}=e_{5}e_{1}f_{10}~~T_{7}=e_{2}e_{8}f_{10},~~T_{10}=e_{5}f_{6}e_{8}$
$\textrm{Obtuse Triangles:}~~T_{6}^{\prime}=e_{1}f_{7}f_{9},~~T_{8}^{\prime}=e_{3}f_{9}f_{6},~~T_{9}^{\prime}=e_{4}f_{10}f_{7},$
For this subcase, we calculate that
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7}).$
Suppose for a contradiction that $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)=\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)+\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)+\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)>4$ . It then follows that all of $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4})$ , $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})$ , $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7})$ , $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})$ , $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{1})$ are nonzero (since $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{i})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}^{\prime})\leq 1$ for each $i\in\{1,7\}$ ). This implies that neither $e_{1}$ nor $e_{2}$ is the sponsor of $T_{1}$ and hence $|\phi(f_{6})|>\phi(e_{1}),\phi(e_{2})$ . But then $\phi(e_{3})\geq|\phi(f_{6})|+\phi(f_{9})|$ . So $\phi(e_{3})\geq\phi(e_{1}),\phi(e_{2})$ but then $\phi(e_{3})\geq\phi(e_{1})\geq 1+|\phi(f_{9})|\geq\phi(f)$ . So $e_{3}$ sponsors $T_{2}$ . Hence $\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)=0$ and therefore $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{1})=0$ , a contradiction.
6.1.5 Proof of Lemma6.1(1e)
This subcase assumes there are no remaining negative edges (that is $e_{5},e_{6},e_{8}$ are all positive). Thus we have positive edges $e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{5},e_{6},e_{8}$ and negative edges $f_{7},f_{9},f_{10}$ . Similarly, here is a breakdown of the acute/obtuse triangles in this case:
$\textrm{All Positive:}~~T_{1}=e_{1}e_{2}e_{6},~~T_{3}=e_{3}e_{4}e_{8},~~T_{10}=e_{5}e_{6}e_{8}$
$\textrm{Acute Triangles:}~~T_{2}=e_{2}e_{3}f_{7},~~T_{4}=e_{4}e_{5}f_{9},~~T_{5}=e_{5}e_{1}f_{10}~~T_{7}=e_{2}e_{8}f_{10},~~T_{8}=e_{3}f_{9}e_{6},$
$\textrm{Obtuse Triangles:}~~T_{6}^{\prime}=e_{1}f_{7}f_{9},~~T_{9}^{\prime}=e_{4}f_{10}f_{7},$
For this subcase, we calculate that
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7}).$
Since $\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)\leq 1$ , we actually find that the sum of the above is at most $3$ .
6.2 Proof of Lemma6.1(2)
Recall that in this case the positive edges include $e_{1}$ , $e_{2}$ , $e_{3}$ , $e_{4},e_{10}$ and the negative edges include $f_{7},f_{9}$ (with the signs of $g_{5},g_{6},g_{8}$ varying among the subcases).
6.2.1 Proof of Lemma6.1(2a)
This subcase assumes the remaining negative edges are $f_{5}$ and $f_{8}$ . Thus we thus have positive edges $e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{6},e_{10}$ and negative edges $f_{5},f_{7},f_{8},f_{9}$ . Similarly, here is a breakdown of the acute/obtuse triangles in this case:
$\textrm{All Positive:}~~T_{1}=e_{1}e_{2}e_{6},$
$\textrm{Acute Triangles:}~~T_{2}=e_{2}e_{3}f_{7},~~T_{3}=e_{3}e_{4}f_{8},~~T_{5}=f_{5}e_{1}e_{10},~~T_{7}=e_{2}f_{8}e_{10},~~T_{8}=e_{3}f_{9}e_{6},~~T_{9}=e_{4}e_{10}f_{7},$
$\textrm{Obtuse Triangles:}~~T_{4}^{\prime}=e_{4}f_{5}f_{9},~~T_{6}^{\prime}=e_{1}f_{7}f_{9},~~T_{10}^{\prime}=f_{5}e_{6}f_{8}$
For this subcase, we calculate that
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e_{10},T_{5})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e_{10},T_{7}).$
Suppose for a contradiction that $\textrm{Val}_{f}(T)=\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)+\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)+\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)>4$ . Recall that $\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)\leq 1$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{i})\leq 1$ for any $i\in\{3,8\}$ . Similarly $\textrm{dem}(e_{4},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{10},f)\leq 1$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{i})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{10},T_{j})\leq 1$ for any $i\in\{3,4\}$ , $j\in\{5,7\}$ . (This already implies the sum of values above is at most $5$ ). Since the sum of values above is strictly greater than $4$ , this implies that all of the following hold:
(i)
there exists $i\in\{4,10\}$ such that both of the terms containing $e_{i}$ are nonzero,
(ii)
$\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3}),~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8})>0$ , and
(iii)
$\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})>0$ .
Since $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8})>0$ , it follows that $\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f_{9})>0$ and hence $e_{6}$ is not the sponsor of $T_{8}$ . Since $e_{6}$ is not the sponsor of $T_{8}$ , it follows by definition of sponsor (and $\phi$ being untied) that $\phi(e_{6})<\max\{\phi(e_{3}),|\phi(f_{9})|\}$ . Since $\phi$ is triangle-covered (in particular for the triangle $T_{4}^{\prime}$ ), we find that $\phi(e_{4})\geq|\phi(f_{5})|+|\phi(f_{9})|$ and hence $\phi(e_{4})\geq\phi(e_{6}).$ Since $\phi$ is triangle-covered (in particular for the triangle $T_{10}^{\prime}$ ), we find that $\phi(e_{6})\geq|\phi(f_{5})|+|\phi(f_{8})|$ and hence $\phi(e_{4})\geq\phi(e_{6})\geq|\phi(f_{8})|.$ It follow that $T_{3}=e_{4}e_{4}f_{8}$ has a sponsor. Since $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3})>0$ by (ii), we find that $e_{3}$ is the sponsor of $T_{3}$ . This implies that $\phi(e_{3})>\phi(e_{4})$ by definition of sponsor.
As $e_{3}$ is the sponsor of $T_{3}$ , we also find that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})=0$ and hence (i) does not hold for $i=4$ . Thus (i) holds for $i=10$ and we have that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{10},T_{5}),\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{10},T_{7})>0$ . Since $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{1},T_{5})>0$ by (iii) and $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{10},T_{5})>0$ , we find that $T_{5}=f_{5}e_{1}e_{10}$ is unsponsored. Hence by definition of sponsor, we find that $|\phi(f_{5})|>\phi(e_{1}),\phi(e_{10})$ . Thus $\phi(e_{4})\geq|\phi(f_{5})|>\phi(e_{10})$ . Since $\phi$ is triangle-covered (in particular for the triangle $T_{6}^{\prime}$ ), we find that $\phi(e_{1})\geq|\phi(f_{7})|+|\phi(f_{9})|$ . Hence $\phi(e_{4})\geq|\phi(f_{7})|$ . Thus $\phi(e_{4})\geq\phi(e_{1})\geq|\phi(f_{7})|$ . It now follows that $e_{4}$ is the sponsor of $T_{9}=e_{4}e_{10}f_{7}$ . Since $f=f_{7}$ , this implies that $\textrm{dem}(e_{10},f)=0$ and hence $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{10},T_{5})=0$ , a contradiction.
6.2.2 Proof of Lemma6.1(2b)
This subcase assumes the only remaining negative edge is $f_{8}$ . In this subcase we thus have positive edges $e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4},e_{5},e_{6},e_{10}$ and negative edges $f_{7},f_{8},f_{9}$ . Similarly, here is a breakdown of the acute/obtuse triangles in this case:
$\textrm{All Positive:}~~T_{1}=e_{1}e_{2}e_{6},~~T_{5}=e_{5}e_{1}e_{10},$
$\textrm{Acute Triangles:}~~T_{2}=e_{2}e_{3}f_{7},~~T_{3}=e_{3}e_{4}f_{8},~~T_{4}=e_{4}e_{5}f_{9},~~T_{7}=e_{2}f_{8}e_{10},~~T_{8}=e_{3}f_{9}e_{6},~~T_{9}=e_{4}e_{10}f_{7},~~T_{10}=e_{5}e_{6}f_{8}$
$\textrm{Obtuse Triangles:}~~T_{6}^{\prime}=e_{1}f_{7}f_{9}$
For this subcase, we calculate that
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{4}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(xz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8}),$
$\displaystyle\textrm{Val}_{f}(yz)$
$\displaystyle=\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{3})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e_{10},T_{7}).$
Since $\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)\leq 1$ , we find that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{3},T_{8})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{2},T_{7})\leq\textrm{dem}(e_{3},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{2},f)\leq 1$ . Similarly since $\textrm{dem}(e_{4},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{10},f)\leq 1$ , we find that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e_{4},T_{3})+\widehat{\textrm{dem}}(e_{10},T_{7})\leq\textrm{dem}(e_{4},f)+\textrm{dem}(e_{10},f)\leq 1$ . Thus the total sum of values above is at most $4$ as desired. This concludes the proof of Lemma 6.1(2b) and hence of Lemma 6.1 and hence of the Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture.
Part II Fractional Stability
7 Proof Overview
Our proof of Theorem 1.6 consists of three main parts (in addition to reusing many of the lemmas from the proof of the Fractional Nash-Williams’ Conjecture). First we prove stability versions of Lemmas 5.2 and 4.29. Second we use these value stability lemmas and the bounds from the discharging lemmas in Section 4 to prove lemmas about the local structure of edges whose charge after discharging is negative or not very positive; here local means of the common neighborhood of their endpoints and structure refers to both graph structure but also the edge-weights in a supposed negative value triangle-covered edge-weighting of the graph $G$ . Third we introduce two additional discharging rules which will be triggered in sequence after the discharging of Section 4 (so this is an example of layered discharging); the first additional rule sends charge from edges with very positive $\textrm{ch}_{F}$ , half of the charge is spread uniformly to incident negative edges and the other half of the charge is spread uniformly to all edges; the second additional rule applies the same discharging scheme to those edges that are very positive after the first additional rule fires. We show using the local structure lemmas that all edges have nonnegative charge after these new additional discharging rules provided the graph is not close to extremal.
The trickiness in needing two additional rules arises as we need not just the local structure of one common neighborhood (which would be roughly $n/2$ vertices, that is half the graph) but rather the extremal structure throughout the entire graph. This becomes quite complex then since while we can with a great deal of effort characterize the common neighborhood of negative edges that are extremal (i.e. do not become very positive after our initial discharging), we need to show such neighborhoods not only admit a nice graph structure but also have negative weights roughly similar (or larger) to that of an extremal edge so as to propagate structure throughout the graph.
7.1 Value Stability
The first step in our proof of Theorem 1.6 is to prove a stability version of Lemma 5.2, namely Lemma 8.7. We delay the statement until the next section as it turns out there are four extremal families of $(x_{i},a_{ij})$ which as we will see can be viewed as step graphons, see Definition 8.5 for the definition of the four families. While the two base families are essentially complete balanced bipartite cuts, the latter two arise when the $x_{i}$ admit symmetry (either reflective or fully symmetric) since then it is possible to average two or even three bipartite cuts to create new extremal families. The stability lemma then shows that if the value is close to the extremal value of $\frac{1}{4}$ , then the step graphon is close to a member of one of these four extremal families.
The next step then is to prove a stability version of Lemma 4.29, namely Lemma 8.10. This stability lemma essentially says that if a $K_{5}$ -free $[0,1]$ -edge-weighted triangle-thin graph on $n$ vertices has value close to $\frac{n^{2}}{4}$ , then it is close to being a blowup of a member of one of the four extremal families from Definition 8.5. In general, it would be possible to show for this type of problem (with the assumption of being $K_{r}$ -free) that a nearly extremal example has a partition into $r-1$ ‘quasi-independent’ sets such that almost all of the transversal $K_{r-1}$ ’s are nearly extremal for the step graphon version of the problem. However, whether that forces the edge weights of the $K_{r-1}$ ’s to have similar structure depends on the specific problem. For our problem, we show that it does and hence the nearly extremal graphs are close to being blowups but it is conceivable that in other similar problems different types of extremal $K_{r-1}$ ’s can simultaneously coexist and the nearly extremal examples would not necessarily need to be a blowup. Given this, we make no attempt to build a general theory of the maximum total edge-weight of $K_{r}$ -free graphs satisfying certain clique weight upper bounds but rather instead proceed to prove just the stability version of our own specific problem ( $K_{5}$ -free triangle-thin).
7.2 Local Structure
Next we proceed to characterize the local structure of extremal edges (those negative edges $f$ with $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|$ ). First we prove some lemmas about $\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)$ ; the first such lemma, Lemma 9.2 characterizes the behavior of this function when one of $x$ and $y$ forms an obtuse triangle with $f$ and the other forms an acute triangle with $f$ ; the second value lemma, Lemma 9.3, characterizes the nature of pairs $xy$ where $\textrm{Val}_{f}(xy)$ is close to its maximum of $2\cdot|\phi(f)|$ . We then use these value lemmas to prove our main structural lemma about the common neighborhood of extremal edges, Lemma 9.5, which essentially says that the common neighborhood is small (close to $n/2$ ) and additionally falls into one of two categories: either $f=uv$ satisfies that both $u$ and $v$ have $\alpha\cdot n$ incident negative edges $f^{\prime}$ with $|\phi(f^{\prime})|\geq 2\cdot|\phi(f)|$ (what we call $\alpha$ -locally-small) or $N(u)\cap N(v)$ admits a cut with at least $(1-\alpha)\cdot\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}}{2}$ negatives $f^{\prime}$ with $|\phi(f^{\prime})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f)|$ (what we call $\alpha$ -bipartite).
7.3 Forcing the Global Structure: Additional Discharging Rules
Finally we proceed with forcing the global extremal structure. First to that end, we prove a “cleaning lemma”, Lemma 10.2, which is useful in forcing structure out from a common neighborhood of an extremal edge $f$ for vertices that are in an obtuse triangle with $f$ .
Finally, we turn towards proving our main fractional stability result. To that end, we need the promised additional layer discharging rules.
Extra Discharging Rules:
Rule 1:
Every negative edge $f=uv$ with $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|$ sends $\frac{1}{2}\cdot\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\cdot\frac{1}{|N^{-}(u)\cup N^{-}(v)|}$ to each edge in $N^{-}(u)\cup N^{-}(v)$ and $\frac{1}{2}\cdot\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\cdot\frac{1}{e(G)}$ to each edge of $G$ . We denote the charge after this rule by $\textrm{ch}_{1}$ .
Rule 2:
Every negative edge $f=uv$ with $\textrm{ch}_{1}(f)\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|$ sends $\frac{1}{2}\cdot\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\cdot\frac{1}{|N^{-}(u)\cup N^{-}(v)|}$ to each edge in $N^{-}(u)\cup N^{-}(v)$ and $\frac{1}{2}\cdot\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\cdot\frac{1}{e(G)}$ to each edge of $G$ . We denote the charge after this rule by $\textrm{ch}_{2}$ .
Recall our main fractional stability result is that if $G$ with $\delta(G)\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon)n$ has no fractional $K_{3}$ -decomposition, then we want to show $(1-\sigma)n$ of the vertices have degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)n$ and the max cut is at least $(1-\sigma)\cdot\frac{n^{2}}{4}$ . Yet if $G$ has no fractional $K_{3}$ -decomposition then by Lemma 4.4, $G$ has a triangle-covered edge-weighting $\phi$ with $|\phi|<0$ and so applying our original and new discharging rules there exists $f^{*}$ with $\textrm{ch}_{2}(f^{*})<0$ . We show that this leads to the extremal structure.
Roughly the proof idea is as follows. We consider a heaviest edge $f_{0}=u_{0}v_{0}$ that is still extremal after Rule 1 (i.e. $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f_{0}),\textrm{ch}_{1}(f_{0})<\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ ). We show all negative edges close to $f_{0}$ in weight that do not discharge in Rules 1 or 2 (including $f_{0}$ itself) are actually $\alpha$ -bipartite (for some small $\alpha$ depending on $\varepsilon$ ).
Thus we consider $N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})$ and case accordingly. If the two sides $A_{0},B_{0}$ of the promised bipartite cut (from $f_{0}$ being $\alpha$ -bipartite) each have very few edges, then that means $G$ already has desired max cut size (namely between $A:=N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})$ and $B:=V(G)\setminus A$ ). Thus it remains to show most vertices have degree close to $3n/4$ . This is already true for most vertices in $N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})$ (since most are incident with many negative edges with weight close to $f_{0}$ , and most of those must not discharge in Rule 1 or 2 as otherwise the whole graph discharges, and so most are $\alpha$ -bipartite). So we carefully choose a ‘typical’ negative edge from the bipartite cut, call if $f_{0}^{\prime}$ , argue its common neighborhood is essentially $B$ and then apply the same argument we made before to show most vertices of $B$ have degree roughly $3n/4$ .
So we instead assume one of the sides, say $A_{0}$ , has a decent number of edges (say $\geq\beta\cdot n^{2}$ for some well-chosen $\beta$ ). We then instead abandon $f_{0}$ and consider a ‘typical’ edge $f_{1}=u_{1}v_{1}$ of the bipartite cut with the additional property that is incident with $\beta\cdot n$ edges inside $A_{0}$ . Most of these neighbors behave ‘normally’ (both with respect to the cut, but also in not being incident with many heavy edges that discharge in Rule 1 or 2) and thus we find many vertices $w$ that form an obtuse triangle with $f_{1}$ (say with $u_{1}w$ is positive and thus has weight roughly twice that of $f_{0}$ ).
We then use one of these obtuse vertices $u_{2}$ (say it is in $A_{1}$ of the new cut $(A_{1},B_{1})$ and our cleaning lemma, Lemma 10.2, to argue that most of $B:=(N(u_{1})\setminus N(v_{1}))\cup B_{1}$ lies inside $N^{-}(u_{2})$ and with edges roughly weight of $f_{0}$ . Since these edges are also $\alpha$ -bipartite, this implies that such vertices have degree at most roughly $n/4$ inside $B$ . Hence they have degree roughly $3n/4$ and setting $A:=V(G)\setminus B$ , we find our desired max cut. Lastly it remains to show most vertices of $A$ have degree at most $3n/4$ which we again achieve by considering $f_{2}=u_{2}v_{1}$ (and finding a well-chosen $v_{1}$ -obtuse vertex $v_{2}$ to apply the cleaning lemma to).
8 Value Stability
Definition 8.1.
A normalized vertex-weighting $\mu$ of a graph $G$ is a function $\mu:V(G)\rightarrow[0,1]$ such that $\sum_{v\in V(G)}\mu(v)=1$ . If $\varphi$ is an edge-weighting of $G$ , then the value of $\varphi$ under $\mu$ ,denoted $|\varphi|_{\mu}$ , is $\sum_{e=uv\in E(G)}\varphi(e)\cdot\mu(u)\cdot\mu(v)$ .
In these terms, Lemma 5.2 says that if $G\cong K_{4}$ , $\mu$ is a normalized vertex weighting of $G$ and $\varphi$ is a triangle-thin $[0,1]$ -edge-weighting of $G$ , then the value of $\varphi$ under $\mu$ is at most $1/4$ . We seek to characterize the pairs of $\mu,\varphi$ that are (close to) extremal for this setting. To that end, let us recall some terminology from the theory of graphons.
Definition 8.2.
A graphon is a symmetric measurable function $W:[0,1]^{2}\rightarrow[0,1]$ and a step graphon is a graphon $W$ that admits a partition of $[0,1]$ into a finite number of intervals $A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}$ such that $W$ is constant on $A_{i}\times A_{j}$ for all $i,j\in[k]$ . We regard a step graphon equivalently as a triple $(G,\mu,\varphi)$ where $G$ is a (finite but possibly with loops) graph, $\mu$ is a normalized vertex-weighting of $G$ , and $\varphi$ is a $[0,1]$ -edge-weighting of $G$ . The equivalence is we let $V(G):=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{k}\}$ , $\mu(v_{i}):=|A_{i}|$ , $\varphi(v_{i}v_{j}):=W(a_{i},a_{j})$ where $a_{i}\in A_{i},a_{j}\in A_{j}$ . We let $|W|:=|\varphi|_{\mu}$ denote the value of $W$ .
Definition 8.3.
We say two step graphons $(G,\mu,\varphi)$ and $(G^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})$ are isomorphic if there exists an isomorphism $\sigma$ from $G$ to $G^{\prime}$ such that $\mu(v)=\mu^{\prime}(\sigma(v))$ for all $v\in V(G)$ and $\varphi(e)=\varphi^{\prime}(\sigma(e))$ for all $e\in E(G)$ . Furthermore, we say two step graphons $(G,\mu,\varphi)$ and $(G^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\varphi^{\prime})$ are $\delta$ -close if there exists an isomorphism $\sigma$ from $G$ to $G^{\prime}$ such that $|\mu(v)-\mu^{\prime}(\sigma(v))|\leq\delta$ for all $v\in V(G)$ and $|\varphi(e)-\varphi^{\prime}(\sigma(e))|\leq\delta$ for all $e\in E(G)$ .
We note that the notion of $\delta$ -close defined above is similar in spirit to what would be the notion of edit distance between step graphons (or weighted edit distance for weighted graphs) except those would more naturally require that for $e=uv$ , we have $|\varphi(e)\cdot\mu(u)\cdot\mu(v)-\varphi^{\prime}(\sigma(e))\cdot\mu^{\prime}(\sigma(v))\cdot\mu^{\prime}(\sigma(u))|\leq\delta$ say (or the same summed over all edges but this is roughly equivalent if we view $v(G)$ as constant). Note that our notion is a stronger assumption. For our purposes, we do not need the more precise but weaker notion as we will show that step graphons that are close to the extremal value for our problem are close in this stronger notion to one of the extremal families.
Returning to our problem, we need some additional definitions. Recall that an edge-weighting $\varphi$ of graph $G$ is triangle-thin if for every triangle $T$ of $G$ , we have that $\sum_{e\in E(T)}\varphi(e)\leq 2$ .
Definition 8.4.
We say a step graphon $(G,\mu,\varphi)$ is triangle-thin if $\varphi$ is a triangle-thin edge-weighting of $G$ . We say a step graphon $(K_{4},\mu,\varphi)$ is matched if $\varphi(e)=\varphi(e^{\prime})$ for every all $e,e^{\prime}\in E(K_{4})$ with $e\cap e^{\prime}=\emptyset$ (i.e. for every matching $e,e^{\prime}$ of $K_{4}$ ).
Now we turn to defining the step graphons that are extremal for our problem as follows.
Definition 8.5.
Let $W=(K_{4},\mu,\varphi)$ be a step graphon and let $x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}$ be an enumeration of $V(K_{4})$ such that $\mu(x_{1})\geq\mu(x_{2})\geq\mu(x_{3})\geq\mu(x_{4})$ . We say $W$ is
•
star-cut if $\mu(x_{1})=1/2$ , and for all distinct $i,j\in[4]$ with $\mu(x_{i}),\mu(x_{j})>0$ we have that $\varphi(x_{i}x_{j})=1$ if $i=1,~j\in\{2,3,4\}$ and $0$ otherwise,
•
cycle-cut if $\mu(x_{1})+\mu(x_{4})=1/2$ , $W$ is matched and $\varphi(x_{1}x_{2})=\varphi(x_{1}x_{3})=1$ and $\varphi(x_{1}x_{4})=0$ ,
•
reflective-cut if $\mu(x_{1})+\mu(x_{4})=1/2$ , $\mu(x_{1})=\mu(x_{2})$ , $W$ is matched, $\varphi(x_{1}x_{2})=1$ and $\varphi(x_{1}x_{3})+\varphi(x_{1}x_{4})=1$ ,
•
quad-cut if $\mu(x_{i})=1/4$ for all $i\in[4]$ , $W$ is matched and $\varphi(x_{1}x_{2})+\varphi(x_{1}x_{3})+\varphi(x_{2}x_{3})=2$ .
