Then it follows from Claim 9.5.7(i) and the remark above that every edge of $J$ is in a $K_{4}$ in $J$ that $E(J[V_{\sigma(i)},V_{\sigma(j)}])\subseteq\mathcal{E}$ for all $\{i,j\}\in\bigg\{\{1,3\},\{2,4\},\{3,4\}\bigg\}$ and that all but at most $\alpha\cdot n^{2}$ edges in $E(J[V_{\sigma(1)},V_{\sigma(2)}])$ are in $\mathcal{E}$ . Moreover, we also find that $m_{23}\neq 1$ and hence whether $W$ is reflective-cut or quad-cut, we find that $\mu(x_{\sigma(2)})+\mu(x_{\sigma(3)})$ . Hence $\bigg||V_{\sigma(2)}^{\prime}|+|V_{\sigma(3)}^{\prime}|-\frac{v(H)}{2}\bigg|\leq 12\sqrt{\beta}\cdot n$ . Similarly $\bigg||V_{\sigma(1)}^{\prime}|+|V_{\sigma(4)}^{\prime}|-\frac{v(H)}{2}\bigg|\leq 12\sqrt{\beta}\cdot n$ . Thus we find that
$\displaystyle|\mathcal{E}|$
$\displaystyle\geq(|V_{\sigma(2)}^{\prime}|+|V_{\sigma(3)}^{\prime}|)(|V_{\sigma(1)}^{\prime}|+|V_{\sigma(4)}^{\prime}|-\sqrt{\beta}\cdot n)-\alpha\cdot n^{2}$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{v(H)}{2}-12\sqrt{\beta}\cdot n\right)\left(\frac{v(H)}{2}-13\sqrt{\beta}\cdot n\right)-\alpha n^{2}$
$\displaystyle\geq\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}}{4}-25\sqrt{\beta}\cdot n\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|-\alpha\cdot n^{2}$
$\displaystyle\geq(1-300\sqrt{\beta}-36\alpha)\cdot\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}}{4}$
where used that all vertices of $J$ are normal, that $v(H)=|N(u)\cap N(v)|\geq\frac{n}{3}$ , and $\alpha\geq 300\sqrt{\beta}$ . But then it follows that $f$ is $(36\alpha+4900\sqrt{\beta})$ -bipartite as desired by fixing any bipartition $(A,B)$ of $N(u)\cap N(v)$ with $V_{\sigma(2)}^{\prime}\cup V_{\sigma(3)}^{\prime}\subseteq A$ and $V_{\sigma(1)}^{\prime}\cup V_{\sigma(4)}^{\prime}\subseteq B$ .
Next suppose that $m_{23}>\frac{1}{2}+\beta$ . Then the symmetric argument (using Claim 9.5.7(ii) instead) to the one given above yields that $f$ is $(36\alpha+4900\sqrt{\beta})$ -bipartite as desired.
Finally we assume that $m_{12},m_{23}\leq\frac{1}{2}+\beta$ (and hence also that $m_{12},m_{23}\geq\frac{1}{2}-\beta$ ). Thus for each $K_{4}$ in $J$ , one of Claim 9.5.7(iii) or (iv) holds for it. We claim that the same outcome (either (iii) or (iv)) holds for all $K_{4}$ in $J$ . Suppose not. Then there exists $w_{3}\in V_{\sigma(3)}^{\prime}$ with $\textrm{dem}(uw_{3},f)\geq 1-6\beta$ (since outcome (iii) holds for some $K_{4}$ ) and similarly there exists $w_{3}^{\prime}\in V_{\sigma(3)}^{\prime}$ with $\textrm{dem}(vw_{3}^{\prime},f)\geq 1-6\beta$ . Since $|V_{\sigma(4)}^{\prime}|>2\sqrt{\beta}\cdot n$ , there exists a vertex $w_{4}\in V_{\sigma(4)}^{\prime}$ such that both $w_{3}w_{4}$ and $w_{3}^{\prime}w_{4}$ are normal (and hence in $E(J)$ ). As remarked before every edge of $J$ is in some copy of $K_{4}$ in $J$ . Since $1-6\beta>\beta$ as $\beta<\frac{1}{12}$ , we find that Claim 9.5.7(iv) does not hold for a copy of $K_{4}$ containing $w_{3}w_{4}$ and hence (iii) holds yielding that $\textrm{dem}(vw_{4},f)\geq 1-6\beta$ . Similarly Claim 9.5.7(iii) does not hold for a copy of $K_{4}$ containing $w_{3}^{\prime}w_{4}$ and hence (iv) holds yielding that $\textrm{dem}(uw_{4},f)\geq 1-6\beta$ . But then $1=|\phi(f)|=\textrm{dem}(uw_{4},f)+\textrm{dem}(vw_{4},f)\geq 2-12\beta>1$ , a contradiction. This proves our claim that the same outcome holds for all $K_{4}$ in $J$ .
We assume without loss of generality that outcome (iii) holds for all $K_{4}$ in $J$ . Then it follows from Claim 9.5.7(iii) and the remark from before that every edge of $J$ is in a $K_{4}$ in $J$ that $E(J[V_{\sigma(i)},V_{\sigma(j)}])\subseteq\mathcal{E}$ for all $\{i,j\}\in\bigg\{\{1,2\},\{1,3\},\{2,4\},\{3,4\}\bigg\}$ . Then an identical argument as the one given for the case $m_{13}\frac{1}{2}+\beta$ yields that $f$ is $(36\alpha+4900\sqrt{\beta})$ -bipartite as desired.
∎
Lemma 9.6 (Extremal Neighborhood Structure - Charge Version).
There exists $\varepsilon_{0}$ such that the following holds for each $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]$ : Let $\alpha\in(0,1)$ and let $\gamma$ such that $2\varepsilon^{1/4}\geq\gamma\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ . Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon)n$ and let $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . If $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\leq\gamma\cdot|\phi(f)|$ , then
$|N(u)\cap N(v)|\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\gamma\right)n$
and $f$ is
(i)
$\alpha$ -locally-small, or
(ii)
$(36\alpha+5000\cdot\gamma^{1/2000})$ -bipartite.
Proof.
We choose $\varepsilon_{0}$ small enough so that $\gamma$ is smaller than the $\varepsilon_{0}$ in Lemma 9.5. Recall that by Lemma 4.21, we have that
$\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\geq\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)=\phi(f)\left(1-\frac{4}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\right)-\frac{4}{n^{2}}\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}~~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime}).$
Let $d:=\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|}{n}$ and $m:=\frac{1}{n}\cdot\max\{|N(u)\setminus N(v)|,~|N(v)\setminus N(u)|\}$ . Since $\delta(G)\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)n$ , we find that $1\geq d\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon$ and $m\leq\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon$ . Also note that $\frac{1-d}{2}\leq m\leq 1-d$ .
For $e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)$ , we find that
$\displaystyle\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e):f^{\prime}\setminus e\not\in N(u)\cap N(v)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})$
$\displaystyle=\sum_{x\not\in N(u)\cap N(v):e\cup\{x\}\textrm{ is a triangle of $G$}}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,e\cup\{x\}).$
$\displaystyle\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot|\{x\not\in N(u)\cap N(v):e\cup\{x\}\textrm{ is a triangle of $G$}\}|$
$\displaystyle\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\max\{|N(u)\setminus N(v)|,|N(v)\setminus N(u)|\}$
$\displaystyle\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot m\cdot n,$
where for the first inequality we used that $\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,e\cup{x})\leq|\phi(f)|$
by Proposition 4.19, the second inequality follows as at exactly one of $u$ or $v$ is an end of $e$ , and the third inequality follows by definition of $m$ .
Hence we calculate that
$\displaystyle\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e):~f^{\prime}\setminus e\not\in N(u)\cap N(v)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})$
$\displaystyle\leq m\cdot n\cdot\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}\textrm{dem}(e,f)$
$\displaystyle\leq m\cdot n\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\cdot|\phi(f)|,$
where we used that $|\{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)|\leq|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ . Substituting this bound yields that (recalling as $f$ is negative that $|\phi(f)|=-\phi(f)$ )
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)$
$\displaystyle\geq\phi(f)\left(1-\frac{(4-4m)}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\right)-\frac{4}{n^{2}}\cdot\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f),$
Since $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\leq\gamma\cdot|\phi(f)|$ , this rearranges to
$\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f)\geq\frac{n^{2}}{4}\cdot|\phi(f)|\cdot\bigg((4-4m)d-1-\gamma\bigg)$
Recall from our proof of Conjecture 4.22, we found that $\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f)\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}}{2}$ and hence combining these inequalities yields
$2d^{2}\geq(4-4m)d-1-\gamma.$
First suppose that $d\geq\frac{9}{10}$ , then $m\leq\frac{1}{10}$ and the right-side above is at least $\frac{36}{10}d-1-\gamma\geq\frac{36}{10}\cdot\frac{9}{10}-1-\gamma\geq\frac{324}{100}-1-\gamma\geq 2.24-\gamma>2$ , a contradiction (since the left-side is at most $2$ as $d\leq 1$ and $\gamma\leq\varepsilon^{1/4}$ is small enough).
So we assume $d<\frac{9}{10}$ . Now $m\leq\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon$ and hence
$\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f)\geq\frac{n^{2}}{4}\cdot|\phi(f)|\cdot\bigg((3-4\varepsilon)d-1-\gamma\bigg),$
and also that
$2d^{2}\geq(3-4\varepsilon)d-1-\gamma.$
Suppose for a contradiction that $d\geq\frac{1}{2}+2\gamma.$ Then $d\in[\frac{1}{2}+2\gamma,~0.9]$ . Consider the function $f(d):=2d^{2}-(3-4\varepsilon)d$ . Note that $f$ is minimized at $4d=3-4\varepsilon$ or equivalently $d=\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon$ . Since this lies inside our range of $d$ (as $\varepsilon$ is small enough), it follows as $f(d)$ is a quadratic function that the maximum of $f$ in this range is at one of the two endpoints. Yet $f(0.9)=2(0.9)^{2}-(3-4\varepsilon)\cdot 0.9\leq 1.62-2.7+3.6\cdot\varepsilon=-1.08+3.6\varepsilon<-1-\gamma$ since $\varepsilon$ is small enough. Similarly
$\displaystyle f\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\gamma\right)$
$\displaystyle=2\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\gamma\right)^{2}-(3-4\varepsilon)\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\gamma\right)$
$\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}+4\gamma+8\gamma^{2}-\frac{3}{2}-6\gamma+2\varepsilon+8\varepsilon\cdot\gamma$
$\displaystyle\leq-1-2\gamma+8\gamma(\gamma+\varepsilon)+2\varepsilon$
$\displaystyle<-1-\gamma,$
where the last two inequalities follow since $\gamma\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ and $\varepsilon$ is small enough. Thus for all $d\in[\frac{1}{2}+2\gamma,~0.9]$ , we find that $f(d)<-1-\gamma$ , a contradiction.
So we assume $d\leq\frac{1}{2}+2\gamma$ . Thus
$n\geq\frac{1}{\frac{1}{2}+2\gamma}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|=\frac{2}{1+4\gamma}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\geq 2\cdot(1-4\gamma)\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|.$
Since $d\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon$ , we find that
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f)$
$\displaystyle\geq\frac{n^{2}}{4}\cdot|\phi(f)|\cdot\left(\frac{3}{2}-8\varepsilon-1-\gamma\right)\geq\frac{n^{2}}{4}\cdot|\phi(f)|\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-9\gamma\right).$
$\displaystyle\geq(1-4\gamma)^{2}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}\cdot|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{1-18\gamma}{2}\geq(1-26\gamma)\cdot\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}}{2}\cdot|\phi(f)|.$
Applying Lemma 9.5 with $26\gamma$ and $\alpha$ yields that $f$ is $\alpha$ -locally-small of $f$ is $(36\alpha+4900\cdot(26\gamma)^{0.0005})$ -bipartite. Since $4900\cdot(26)^{0.0005}\leq 4908\leq 5000$ , we find that $f$ is $(36\alpha+5000\cdot\gamma^{1/2000})$ -bipartite as desired.
∎
10 Forcing the Global Structure
Finally we proceed with forcing the global extremal structure.
10.1 Cleaning Lemma
Definition 10.1.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices and let $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . Let $x\in N(u)\cap N(v)$ be $u$ -obtuse. We say $z\in N(u)\setminus N(v)$ is $\alpha$ -unclean for $x$ with respect to $f$ if either
(a)
$xz\not\in E(G)$ , or
(b)
$\sum_{e\in E^{-}(G)\cap\{xz,uz\}}|\phi(e)|<(1-\alpha)\cdot\phi(ux)$ .
We say $x$ is $\alpha$ -unclean with respect to $f$ if there exist at least $\alpha\cdot n$ vertices $z$ that are $\alpha$ -unclean for $x$ with respect to $f$ ; we say $x$ is $\alpha$ -clean with respect to $f$ if it is not $\alpha$ -unclean.
Lemma 10.2 (Cleaning).
Let $\varepsilon\in(0,\frac{1}{4}]$ . Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)n$ and let $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . If there exist at least $\alpha\cdot n$ vertices in $N(u)\cap N(v)$ that are $\alpha$ -unclean with respect to $f$ , then $\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)\geq|\phi(f)|\cdot(4\alpha^{3}-4\varepsilon)$ .
Proof.
Recall that by Lemma 4.21, we have that
$\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)=\phi(f)\left(1-\frac{4}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\right)-\frac{4}{n^{2}}\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e)}~~\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime}).$
Yet
$\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e):f^{\prime}\setminus e\not\in N(u)\cap N(v)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})=\sum_{z\not\in N(u)\cap N(v):e\cup\{z\}\textrm{ is a triangle of $G$}}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,e\cup\{z\}).$
Suppose that $x\in N(u)\cap N(v)$ is $u$ -obtuse and $\alpha$ -unclean with respect to $f$ . Then
$\displaystyle\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(ux):f^{\prime}\setminus ux\not\in N(u)\cap N(v)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(ux,f^{\prime})$
$\displaystyle=\sum_{z\not\in N(u)\cap N(v):uxz\textrm{ is a triangle of $G$}}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(ux,uxz).$
$\displaystyle\leq\textrm{dem}(ux,f)\cdot(|N(u)\setminus N(v)|-\alpha^{2}\cdot n)$
$\displaystyle\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot(|N(u)\setminus N(v)|-\alpha^{2}\cdot n)\}$
$\displaystyle\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\left(n\left(\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon\right)-\alpha^{2}\cdot n\right)$
$\displaystyle=|\phi(f)|\cdot n\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon-\alpha^{2}\right),$
where we used that $|N(u)\setminus N(v)|\leq n\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon\right)$ since $\delta(G)\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon)n$ .
Since there are at least $\alpha\cdot n$ vertices in $N(u)\cap N(v)$ that are $\alpha$ -unclean with respect to $f$ , we find that
$\displaystyle\sum_{e\in T^{+}_{G,\phi}(f)}~~\sum_{f^{\prime}\in T^{-}_{G,\phi}(e):~f^{\prime}\setminus e\not\in N(u)\cap N(v)}\widehat{\textrm{dem}}_{f}(e,f^{\prime})\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\bigg(|N(u)\cap N(v)|\cdot n\left(\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon\right)-\alpha^{3}n^{2}\bigg).$
Substituting this into the equation from Lemma 4.21 yields that
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)$
$\displaystyle\geq\phi(f)\left(1-\frac{3-4\varepsilon}{n}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\right)+4\alpha^{3}-\frac{4}{n^{2}}\cdot\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f).$
We let $H:=\mathrm{Filter}_{G,\phi}(f)$ and define an edge-weighting $\varphi$ on $E(H)$ as $\varphi(g):=\frac{\textrm{Val}_{f}(g)}{2\cdot|\phi(f)|}$ for each $g\in E(H)$ . Then it follows from Proposition 4.27 that $\varphi$ is a $[0,1]$ -edge-weighting of $H$ . Similarly, it follows from Lemma 4.31 that $\varphi$ is triangle-thin. Hence by Lemma 4.29, we have that $|\varphi|\leq\frac{v(H)^{2}}{4}$ . Since $v(H)=|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ , it follows that
$\textrm{ch}^{\prime\prime}_{F}(f)=\sum_{g\in E(G[N(u)\cap N(v)])}\textrm{Val}_{f}(g)=2\cdot|\phi(f)|\cdot|\varphi|\leq|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}.$
Let $d:=\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|}{n}$ . Substituting this and combining the previous inequalities yields that
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)$
$\displaystyle\geq-|\phi(f)|\cdot\bigg(1-(3-4\varepsilon)d\bigg)+4\cdot|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot d^{2}+4\alpha^{3}=|\phi(f)|\cdot\bigg(-1+(3-4\varepsilon)d-2d^{2}+4\alpha^{3}\bigg).$
The derivative of $-2d^{2}+(3-4\varepsilon)d-1$ is $-4d+3-4\varepsilon$ . Since $1\geq d\geq\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon$ as $\delta(G)\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon)n$ , the quadratic function is minimized on this range at which ever end point is larger (since the maximum is at $d=\frac{3-4\varepsilon}{4}=\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon$ which lies inside the range). At $d=1$ , the function is $-4\varepsilon$ . At $d=\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon$ , the function evaluates to $-4\varepsilon$ . Thus we find that
$\displaystyle\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)\geq|\phi(f)|\cdot\bigg(4\alpha^{3}-4\varepsilon\bigg),$
as desired.
∎
For our main proof, we need the following corollary of Lemma 10.2.
Corollary 10.3.
Let $\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{4}]$ . Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)n$ . If $f=uv$ is a negative edge of $G$ with $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)$ , then there exist at most $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\cdot n$ vertices in $N(u)\cap N(v)$ that are $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}$ -unclean with respect to $f$ .
Proof.
Follows from Lemma 10.2 with $\alpha=2\cdot\sqrt{\varepsilon}^{1/6}$ .
∎
For our main proof, we also need a lower bound on the final charges of negative edges as follows.
Lemma 10.4.
Let $\varepsilon\in[0,\frac{1}{4}]$ . Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)n$ . If $f=uv$ is a negative edge of $G$ , then $\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)\geq-|\phi(f)|\cdot 4\varepsilon$ .
Proof.
Follows from Lemma 10.2 with $\alpha=0$ .
∎
10.2 Fractional Stability Proof
It is mildly convenient in our next major proofs if the bipartitions from $\alpha$ -bipartiteness are balanced. Hence the following definitions. Since technically a balanced bipartition would necessitate the use of floors and ceilings (as in the following definition) but these do not materially affect the proof (which lies in the asymptotic setting), in our proofs in this section we will omit the floors and ceilings for readability.
Definition 10.5.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices. Let $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ . Let $P=(A,B)$ be a bipartition of $N(u)\cap N(v)$ . We say a pair $a\in A$ , $b\in B$ is $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}$ if $ab\in E_{\phi}^{-}(G)$ and $|\phi(ab)|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(uv)|$ . We say $\mathcal{P}$ is an $\alpha$ -bipartition for $f$ if at least $(1-\alpha)\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|^{2}}{4}$ ; we say $\mathcal{P}$ is an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition for $f$ if in addition $|A|=\left\lfloor\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|}{2}\right\rfloor$ and $|B|=\left\lceil\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|}{2}\right\rceil$ . We say a vertex $w\in A\cup B$ is $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}$ if it is in at least $(1-\sqrt{\alpha})\cdot\frac{\lfloor|N(u)\cap N(v)|\rfloor}{2}$ $\alpha$ -normal pairs. We say $f$ is $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartite if there exists an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition for $f$ .
The following proposition shows the two concepts are functionally equivalent.
Proposition 10.6.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices. Let $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ and $\alpha\in(0,1)$ . If $f$ is $\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}$ -bipartite, then it is $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartite.
Proof.
Since $f$ is $\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}$ -bipartite, there exists an $\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}$ -bipartition $\mathcal{P}=(A,B)$ of $f$ . We assume without loss of generality that $|A|\leq|B|$ . Let $m:=|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ . We claim that $|A|\geq(1-\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{2}})\cdot\frac{m}{2}$ . Suppose not. Then the number of $\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}$ -normal pairs for $\mathcal{P}$ is at most $|A|\cdot|B|<\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\cdot\frac{m^{2}}{4}=(1-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2})\cdot\frac{m^{2}}{4}$ , a contradiction.
Let $S\subseteq B$ with $|S|=\left\lfloor\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|}{2}\right\rfloor-|A|$ . Hence $|S|\leq\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\frac{m}{2}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}\cdot n$ . Let $\mathcal{P}^{\prime}=(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})$ with $A^{\prime}:=A\cup S$ and $B^{\prime}:=B\setminus S$ . It follows that $\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$ is an $(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}+\frac{\alpha}{2})$ -balanced-bipartition of $f$ . Since $\alpha\leq 1$ , $\mathcal{P}^{\prime}$ is also then an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition of $f$ as desired.
∎
We also require the next proposition asserting there are not too many abnormal vertices for $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition.
Proposition 10.7.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices. Let $f=uv$ be a negative edge of $G$ and $\alpha\in(0,1)$ . If $\mathcal{P}$ is an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition for $f$ , then there are at most $\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot n$ vertices in $N(u)\cap N(v)$ that are not $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}$ .
Proof.
Let $m:=|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ and let $(A,B):=\mathcal{P}$ . Then there are most $\alpha\cdot\frac{m^{2}}{4}$ pairs $a\in B,b\in B$ such that $ab$ is not $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}$ . Hence there are at most $2\alpha\cdot\frac{m^{2}}{4}\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot n}$ vertices in $N(u)\cap N(v)$ that are not $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}$ . But this is at most $\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot n$ as desired.
∎
Here is another useful proposition.
Proposition 10.8.
Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph. If a negative edge $f=uv$ of $G$ is $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartite, then there are at most $(\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\alpha})\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ vertices that are $u$ -obtuse with respect to $f$ .
Proof.
Suppose not. Let $m=|N(u)\cap N(v)|$ . Let $S$ be the set of vertices that are $u$ -obtuse with respect to $f$ . Thus $|S|>(\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\alpha})\cdot m$ . Let $S_{A}:=S\cap A$ and $S_{B}:=S\cap B$ . Since $|A|=|B|=m$ , it follows $|S_{A}|,|S_{B}|>\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot m$ and hence $|S_{A}|\cdot|S_{B}|>\alpha\cdot m^{2}$ . But for each pair $a\in S_{A},b\in S_{b}$ , we find that $ab$ is a positive edge (since $av$ and $bv$ are negative as $a$ and $b$ are $u$ -obtuse and $\phi$ is triangle-covered). But then $ab$ is not $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}$ . So there are at least $\alpha\cdot m^{2}$ pairs that are not $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}$ , contradicting that $\mathcal{P}$ is an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition.
∎
We are now prepared to our main fractional stability result which is a slightly stronger version of Theorem 1.6 (as it asserts that our specific discharging works).
Theorem 10.9.
There exists $\varepsilon_{0}$ such that the following holds for all $\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}]$ : Let $\sigma:=\varepsilon^{1/20000}$ . Let $(G,\phi)$ be a nonzero untied triangle-covered edge-weighted graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)n$ . If there exists some edge $f^{*}$ of $G$ such that $\textrm{ch}_{2}(f^{*})<0$ , then both of the following hold:
(i)
at least $(1-\sigma)n$ vertices have degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)n$ , and
(ii)
the maximum cut of $G$ is at least $(1-\sigma)\cdot\frac{n^{2}}{4}$ .
Proof.
Suppose not. Let $\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\{f\in E^{-}_{\phi}(G):~\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|\}$ , that is the set of edges that discharge in Rule 1. Let $\mathcal{E}_{1}:=\{f\in E^{-}_{\phi}(G):~\textrm{ch}_{1}(f)\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|\}$ , that is the set of edges that that discharge in Rule 2. Let $\mathcal{E}_{2}:=E(G)\setminus(\mathcal{E}_{0}\cup\mathcal{E}_{1})$ . Since there exists $f^{*}$ with $\textrm{ch}_{2}(f^{*})<0$ , we find that $\mathcal{E}_{2}\neq\emptyset$ . Let $f_{0}=u_{0}v_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}$ such that $|\phi(f_{0})|$ is maximized.
Let $\mathcal{Q}:=\{f\in E^{-}_{\phi}(G):|\phi(f)|>|\phi(f_{0})|\}$ . We let $\mathcal{H}(\gamma):=\{E^{-}_{\phi}(G):|\phi(f)|\geq\gamma\}$ and $\mathcal{H}:=\mathcal{H}(\frac{3}{4}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|)$ . Let $\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{H}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{2}$ . Note by our choice of $f_{0}$ , we have that $\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{0}\cup\mathcal{E}_{1}$ . Thus $\mathcal{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}$ . Since $\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}=\emptyset$ by definition, we find that each $f\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}$ discharges in either Rule 1 or Rule 2.
Claim 10.9.1.
$|\mathcal{Q}|\leq|\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}|\leq 6\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot n^{2}$ .
Proof. Suppose not. Since each $f\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}$ discharges in either Rule 1 or Rule 2, we have that each such $f\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}$ sends to $f^{*}$ an amount of charge at least
$\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{e(G)}\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot\frac{3}{4}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|,$
where we used that $|\phi(f)|\geq\frac{3}{4}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ by definition of $\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}$ and that $2\cdot e(G)\leq n^{2}$ . Note that $|\phi(f^{*})|\leq|\phi(f_{0})|$ as otherwise $\textrm{ch}_{2}(f^{*})\geq 0$ , a contradiction. But then $f^{*}$ receives in the additional discharging rules an amount of charge at least
$|Q|\cdot\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{2}}\cdot\frac{3}{4}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\geq 4\varepsilon\cdot|\phi(f^{*})|.$
Yet by Lemma 10.4, $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f^{*})\geq-4\varepsilon\cdot|\phi(f^{*})|$ and hence $\textrm{ch}_{2}(f^{*})\geq 0$ , a contradiction.
$\Diamond$
Let $S$ denote the set of vertices $v\in V(G)$ such that $v$ is incident with at least $3\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n$ edges of $\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}$ . It follows from Claim 10.9.1 that $|S|\leq 4\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n$ . Let $G^{\prime}$ be the graph with $V(G^{\prime}):=V(G)\setminus S$ and $E(G^{\prime}):=G[V(G^{\prime})]\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}$ .
We now prove a very useful claim characterizing the structure of edges in $\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap H$ as follows. To that end, for the rest of the proof let $\alpha:=\varepsilon^{1/18000}$ .
Claim 10.9.2.
If $f=uv\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}$ , then both of the following hold:
(i)
$|N(u)\cap N(v)|\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n$ and hence $d_{G}(u),d_{G}(v)\leq\left(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n$ , and
(ii)
$f$ is $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartite.
Proof. Suppose not. Since $\textrm{ch}^{\prime}_{F}(f)\leq\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\leq\textrm{ch}_{1}(f)\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|$ , we have by Lemma 9.6 $\gamma_{\ref{lem:NeighborhoodStructure2}}:=2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4})$ that $N(u)\cap N(v)|\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n$ and hence (i) holds.
Let $\gamma:=\varepsilon^{1/4}$ . Now we also apply Lemma 9.6 with $\gamma_{\ref{lem:NeighborhoodStructure2}}:=\gamma$ and $\alpha_{\ref{lem:NeighborhoodStructure2}}:=4\gamma$ to find that $f$ is $\gamma$ -locally-small or $(144\gamma+5000\cdot\gamma^{1/2000})$ -bipartite. First suppose the latter holds. Since $144\gamma+5000\cdot\gamma^{1/2000}\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/9000}$ as $\varepsilon$ is small enough, we then find that $f$ is $\frac{1}{2}\cdot\varepsilon^{1/9000}$ -bipartite and hence by Proposition 10.6, $f$ is $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartite, that is (ii) holds and thus the whole claims holds, a contradiction.
Hence we assume $f$ is $4\gamma$ -locally-small, that is $f$ is incident with at least $4\gamma\cdot n$ negative edges $f^{\prime}$ with $|\phi(f^{\prime})|\geq 2\cdot|\phi(f)|$ . Since $|\phi(f)|>\frac{1}{2}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ as $f\in\mathcal{H}$ , it follows that each such $f^{\prime}\in Q$ . Let $R$ be the set of edges incident with $f$ that are in $Q$ ; hence $|R|\geq 4\gamma\cdot n$ . Let $R^{\prime}:=\{f^{\prime}\in R:\textrm{ch}_{F}(f^{\prime})\geq 2\gamma\cdot|\phi(f^{\prime})|\}$ .
First suppose that $|R^{\prime}|\geq 2\gamma\cdot n$ . But then each $f^{\prime}\in R^{\prime}$ sends in Rule $1$ at least $2\gamma\cdot|\phi(f^{\prime})|\cdot\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2n}$ charge to $f$ . Since $|\phi(f^{\prime})|\geq 2\cdot|\phi(f)|$ , this implies that $f$ receives charge under Rule 1 of at least
$(2\gamma\cdot n)\cdot\frac{2\gamma\cdot 2\cdot|\phi(f)|}{4n}=2\gamma^{2}\cdot|\phi(f)|=2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|\geq(4\varepsilon+\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot|\phi(f)|,$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ is small enough. But $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\geq-4\varepsilon\cdot|\phi(f)|$ by Lemma 10.4. Thus we find that $\textrm{ch}_{1}(f)\geq\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)+(4\varepsilon+\sqrt{\varepsilon})\cdot|\phi(f)|\geq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|$ , contradicting that $f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}$ .
So we assume $|R^{\prime}|\leq 2\gamma\cdot n$ . Thus $|R\setminus R^{\prime}|\geq 2\gamma\cdot n$ . By Lemma 9.6 with $\gamma_{\ref{lem:NeighborhoodStructure2}}:=2\gamma$ and $\alpha_{\ref{lem:NeighborhoodStructure2}}:=\frac{1}{200}$ , we have that for each $f^{\prime}\in R\setminus R^{\prime}$ that $f^{\prime}$ is $\frac{1}{200}$ -locally-small or $(\frac{36}{200}+5000\cdot\gamma^{1/2000})$ -bipartite. First suppose there exists $f^{\prime}=u^{\prime}v^{\prime}\in R\setminus R^{\prime}$ that is $(\frac{36}{200}+5000\cdot\gamma^{1/2000})$ -bipartite and hence is $\frac{1}{5}$ -bipartite since $\varepsilon$ is small enough. Thus by definition, there exist at least $\frac{4}{5}\cdot\frac{|N(u^{\prime})\cap N(v^{\prime})|^{2}}{4}$ negative edges $ab$ with $|\phi(ab)|\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot|\phi(f^{\prime})|$ . But $|\phi(f^{\prime})|\geq 2\cdot|\phi(f)|\geq\frac{3}{2}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ since $|\phi(f)|\geq\frac{3}{4}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ s $f\in\mathcal{H}$ ; hence $\frac{4}{5}\cdot|\phi(f^{\prime})|\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot\frac{3}{2}|\phi(f_{0})|>|\phi(f_{0})|$ . Thus we find that $|Q|\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot\frac{|N(u^{\prime})\cap N(v^{\prime})|^{2}}{4}\geq\frac{4}{5}\cdot\frac{(n/3)^{2}}{4}\geq\frac{n^{2}}{45}$ , contradicting Claim 10.9.1 since $\varepsilon$ is small enough.
So we assume every edge in $R\setminus R^{\prime}$ is $\frac{1}{200}$ -locally-small. Recalling crucially that locally small means both endpoints are incident with many edges that are more negative, we find there are at least $2\gamma n$ vertices each incident with at least $\frac{n}{200}$ edges in $Q$ (noting that for each vertex $y$ only one of $u^{\prime}y$ or $v^{\prime}y$ is in $R$ as $\phi$ is triangle-covered). Thus $|Q|\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\gamma n\cdot\frac{n}{200}\geq\frac{\gamma}{200}\cdot n^{2}>6\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot n^{2}$ contradicting Claim 10.9.1 where we used that $\varepsilon$ is small enough such for this inequality to hold.
$\Diamond$
We also deduce the following claim that more characterizes the common neighborhood of negative edges in $\mathcal{E}_{2}$ with slightly larger negative weight than $\frac{3}{4}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ as follows.
Claim 10.9.3.
Suppose that $f=uv\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}$ with $|\phi(f)|\geq\frac{9}{10}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ . If $\mathcal{P}$ is an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition of $f$ and $\mathcal{N}$ denotes the set of vertices of $N(u)\cap N(v)\cap V(G^{\prime})$ that are both $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}=(V_{1},V_{2})$ and are not $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}$ -unclean with respect to $f$ , then all of the following hold:
(i)
$|(N(u)\cap N(v))\setminus\mathcal{N}|\leq(\sqrt{\alpha}+6\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})\cdot n,$
(ii)
for each $i\in\{1,2\}$ and $w\in V_{i}\cap\mathcal{N}$ , we have that
$\bigg|N_{G^{\prime}}(w)\cap V_{3-i}\cap\mathcal{N}\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f)|\bigg)\bigg|\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-10\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n,$
(iii)
every vertex in $\mathcal{N}$ has degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ ,
(iv)
if $u\in V(G^{\prime})$ , then for each vertex $w\in\mathcal{N}$ that is $u$ -obtuse such that $|\phi(vw)|\geq\frac{9}{10}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ , we have that
$\bigg|N_{G^{\prime}}(w)\cap(N(u)\setminus N(v))\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-\alpha)\cdot(\phi(f)|+|\phi(vw)|)-|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)\bigg|\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)\cdot n.$
Proof. By Proposition 10.7, at most $\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot n$ vertices of $N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})$ are not $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}$ . By Corollary 10.3 since $\textrm{ch}_{F}(f)\leq\sqrt{\varepsilon}\cdot|\phi(f)|$ as $f\in\mathcal{E}_{2}$ , there are most $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\cdot n$ vertices in $N(u)\cap N(v)$ that $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}$ -unclean with respect to $f$ . Since $|V(G)\setminus V(G^{\prime})|=|S|\leq 4\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n$ , combining all this we find that
$|(N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0}))\setminus\mathcal{N}_{0}|\leq\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot n+4\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n+2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\cdot n,$
which proves (i).
Next we proceed towards proving (ii). Without loss of generality let $w\in V_{1}\cap\mathcal{N}$ . Thus for at least $(1-\alpha)\cdot\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|}{2}$ vertices $w^{\prime}\in V_{2}$ we have that $ww^{\prime}$ is $\alpha$ -normal; recall this means that $ww^{\prime}\in E^{-}_{\phi}(G)$ and $|\phi(ww^{\prime})|\geq(1-\alpha)|\phi(f)|\geq\frac{3}{4}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ and hence $ww^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}$ . Also since $w\in V(G^{\prime})$ , we have that $w\not\in S$ and hence $w$ is incident with at most $3\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n$ edges of $\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}\setminus\mathcal{E}_{2}$ . Thus we calculate using (i) that
$\displaystyle\bigg|\bigg\{w^{\prime}\in$
$\displaystyle N_{G^{\prime}}(w)\cap V_{2}\cap\mathcal{N}:|\phi(ww^{\prime})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f)|\bigg\}\bigg|$
$\displaystyle\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot\frac{|N(u)\cap N(v)|}{2}-3\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n-|(N(u)\cap N(v))\setminus\mathcal{N}|$
$\displaystyle\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{4}-\varepsilon\right)n-3\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n-(\sqrt{\alpha}+6\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})\cdot n$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-10\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n$
which proves (ii).
Since $\varepsilon$ is small enough, it follows from (ii) that for each $w\in\mathcal{N}$ , we have that $|N_{G^{\prime}}(w)|>0$ and hence there exists $w^{\prime}\in N_{G^{\prime}}(w)$ ; but then by Claim 10.9.2 applied to $ww^{\prime}$ , we find that $w$ has degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ . This proves (iii).
Finally we proceed towards proving (iv). Let $w\in\mathcal{N}$ such that $w$ is $u$ -obtuse and $vw\in E(G^{\prime})$ . Since $w\in\mathcal{N}$ , we have by definition that $w$ is not $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}$ -unclean for $f$ . Let $Z$ denote the set of vertices in $N(u)\setminus N(v)$ that are $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}$ -unclean for $w$ with respect to $f$ . Recall by definition then $|Z|\leq 2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\cdot n$ . Recall also that $z\in Z$ means either that $wz\not\in E(G)$ or $\sum_{e\in E^{-}(G)\cap\{wz,uz\}}|\phi(e)|<(1-\alpha)\cdot\phi(uw)$ .
Since $vw$ is negative as $w$ is $u$ -obtuse and by assumption $|\phi(vw)|\geq\frac{9}{10}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ , we find as $\phi$ is triangle-covered that
$\phi(uw)\geq|\phi(vw)|+|\phi(f)|\geq\frac{9}{5}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|.$
Let $Y$ denote the set of vertices $y\in N(u)\setminus N(v)$ such that $uy\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}$ or $wy\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}$ . Since $u,w\in\mathcal{N}$ , we have that $|Y|\leq 8\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\cdot n$ . Consider $w^{\prime}\in(N(u)\setminus N(v))\setminus(S\cup Y\cup Z)$ .
We claim that $ww^{\prime}\in E(G^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-\alpha)\cdot(\phi(f)|+|\phi(vw)|)-|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)$ . Since $w^{\prime}\not\in S$ , we have $w^{\prime}\in V(G^{\prime})$ . Since $w^{\prime}\not\in Y$ , we find that $uy,wy\not\in\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}$ and hence if $uy\in\mathcal{H}$ , then $uy\in\mathcal{H}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}$ and so $|\phi(uy)|\leq|\phi(f_{0})|$ (and similarly for $wy$ ). On the other hand since $w^{\prime}\not\in Z$ , we find that $ww^{\prime}\in E(G)$ and furthermore that
$\sum_{e\in E^{-}(G)\cap\{ww^{\prime},uw^{\prime}\}}|\phi(e)|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot\phi(uw)\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot\frac{9}{5}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|>\frac{7}{4}\cdot|\phi(f_{0}),$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ (and hence $\alpha$ ) is small enough. Thus it follows from our comments above that $ww^{\prime},uw^{\prime}\in E^{-}(G)$ and furthermore since $|\phi(uw^{\prime})|\leq|\phi(f_{0})|$ as noted above, then we have that
$\displaystyle|\phi(ww^{\prime})|$
$\displaystyle\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(uw)|-|\phi(uw^{\prime})|$
$\displaystyle\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot(\phi(f)|+|\phi(vw)|)-|\phi(f_{0})|$
$\displaystyle\geq(1-\alpha)\frac{4}{5}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$
$\displaystyle\geq\frac{3}{4}|\phi(f_{0})|,$
where the last inequality follows since $\alpha$ is small enough and hence $ww^{\prime}\in E(G^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-\alpha)\cdot(\phi(f)|+|\phi(vw)|)-|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)$ as claimed.
Note that since $|N(u)\cap N(v)|\leq(\frac{1}{2}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ and $|N(u)|\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon)n$ , we find that $|N(u)\setminus N(v)|\geq(\frac{1}{4}-4\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ . Thus we calculate that
$\displaystyle\bigg|N_{G^{\prime}}(w)$
$\displaystyle\cap(N(u)\setminus N(v))\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-\alpha)\cdot(\phi(f)|+|\phi(vw)|)-|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)\bigg|$
$\displaystyle\geq|N(u)\setminus N(v)|-|S|-|Y|-|Z|$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-4\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n-4\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}n-8\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\cdot n-2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\cdot n$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)\cdot n,$
which proves (iv).
$\Diamond$
Since $f_{0}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}$ , it follows that Claim 10.9.2 applies to $f_{0}$ and hence we find that $|N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})|\leq(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ and $f_{0}$ is $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartite. Thus by definition there exists an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition $\mathcal{P}_{0}=(A_{0},B_{0})$ of $N(u_{0})\cap N(v)$ . We let $\mathcal{N}_{0}$ denote the set of vertices in $N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})\cap V(G^{\prime})$ that are $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}_{0}$ and are not $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}$ -unclean with respect to $f_{0}$ .
For the rest of the proof let $\beta:=6\sqrt{\alpha}$ .
Claim 10.9.4.
$\max\{e(G[A_{0}],e(G[B_{0}])\}\geq\beta\cdot n^{2}$ .
Proof. Suppose not, that is $e(G[A_{0}]),e(G[B_{0}])<\beta\cdot n^{2}$ . By Claim 10.9.3, we find that every vertex of $\mathcal{N}_{0}$ has degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ . Recall that $|N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})|\leq(\frac{1}{2}+2\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ and hence $|N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})|^{2}\leq(\frac{1}{4}+6\sqrt{\varepsilon})n^{2}$ . Thus we calculate that
$e(G[A_{0}\cup B_{0}])\leq|A_{0}|\cdot|B_{0}|+2\beta\cdot n^{2}\leq\frac{|N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})|^{2}}{4}+2\beta\cdot n^{2}\leq\left(\frac{1}{16}+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}+2\beta\right)\cdot n^{2}.$
We now consider the bipartition $(A,B)$ of $V(G)$ with $A:=A_{0}\cup B_{0}$ and $B:=V(G)\setminus A$ . Note that $|A|=|N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})|\geq(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon)n$ . Since $\delta(G)\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon)n$ , it follows that
$\displaystyle e(G[A,B])$
$\displaystyle\geq\delta(G)\cdot|A|-2\cdot e(G[A])$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)n\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon\right)n-2\left(\frac{1}{16}+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}+2\beta\right)\cdot n^{2}$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-6\sqrt{\varepsilon}-4\beta\right)\cdot n^{2}$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-\sigma\right)n^{2},$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ is small enough.
Let $T_{A_{0}}:=\{a\in A_{0}:|N_{G}(a)\cap A_{0}|>16\beta\cdot n\}$ and $T_{B_{0}}:=\{b\in B_{0}:|N_{G}(b)\cap B_{0}|>16\beta\cdot n\}$ . Since $e(G[A_{0}])\leq\beta\cdot n^{2}$ , we find $|T_{A_{0}}|\leq\frac{n}{8}$ ; similarly since $e(G[B_{0}])\leq\beta\cdot n^{2}$ , we find $|T_{B_{0}}|\leq\frac{n}{8}$ . Note that
$\displaystyle|(A_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}_{0})\setminus T_{A_{0}}|$
$\displaystyle\geq|A_{0}|-|(N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0}))\setminus\mathcal{N}_{0}|-|T_{A_{0}}|$
$\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot|N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})|-(\sqrt{\alpha}+6\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})\cdot n-4\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n-\frac{n}{8}$
$\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon\right)n-\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot n-4\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n-\frac{n}{8}$
$\displaystyle>0,$
where in the first inequality we used Claim 10.9.3(i) and the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ (and hence $\alpha$ ) are small enough.
Thus there exists $u_{0}^{\prime}\in(A_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}_{0})\setminus T_{A_{0}}$ . Let
$U_{0}:=\bigg|\bigg\{w^{\prime}\in N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap B_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}:|\phi(u_{0}w^{\prime})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg\}\bigg|.$
Since $u_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}$ , we have by Claim 10.9.3(ii) that
$\displaystyle|U_{0}\setminus T_{B_{0}}|$
$\displaystyle\geq|U_{0}|-|T_{B_{0}}|$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-10\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n-\frac{n}{8}$
$\displaystyle>0,$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ (and hence $\alpha$ ) are small enough.
Then there exists $v_{0}^{\prime}\in U_{0}\setminus T_{B_{0}}$ . Since $v_{0}^{\prime}\not\in T_{B_{0}}$ we have that $|N_{G}(v_{0}^{\prime})\cap B_{0}|\leq 16\beta\cdot n$ . Since $v_{0}^{\prime}\in N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{0}^{\prime})$ , we find that $u_{0}^{\prime}v_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}$ . Let $f_{0}^{\prime}=u_{0}^{\prime}v_{0}^{\prime}$ . Since $v_{0}^{\prime}\in U_{0}$ , we have that $|\phi(f_{0}^{\prime})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\geq\frac{9}{10}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ is small enough. Now we calculate that
$|N(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap N(v_{0}^{\prime})\cap B|\geq|N(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap N(v_{0}^{\prime})|-|N(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap A_{0}|-|N(v_{0}^{\prime})\cap B_{0}|\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\varepsilon-32\beta\right)n.$
Notice that $|B|=n-|A|\leq(\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon)n$ . Hence
$|B\setminus(N(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap N(v_{0}^{\prime}))|=|B|-|N(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap N(v_{0}^{\prime})\cap B|\leq(4\varepsilon+32\beta)n.$
By Claim 10.9.2 since $f_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}$ , we find that $|N(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap N(v_{0}^{\prime})|\leq(\frac{1}{2}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ and that $f_{0}^{\prime}$ is $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartite. Let $\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\prime}=(A_{0}^{\prime},B_{0}^{\prime})$ be an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition for $f_{0}^{\prime}$ . Let $\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\prime}$ be the set of vertices of $N(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap N(v_{0}^{\prime})\cap V(G^{\prime})$ that are $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\prime}$ and are not $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}$ -unclean with respect to $f_{0}$ . By Claim 10.9.3(i) since $f_{0}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}$ and $|\phi(f_{0}^{\prime})|\geq\frac{9}{10}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ , we have that $|(N(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap N(v_{0}^{\prime}))\setminus\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\prime}|\leq(\sqrt{\alpha}+6\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})\cdot n$ . Similarly by Claim 10.9.3(iii), we have that every vertex in $\mathcal{N}_{0}^{\prime}$ has degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ . Hence at most $\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot n+4\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4}\cdot n$ of the vertices of $N(u_{0}^{\prime})\cap N(v_{0}^{\prime})\cap B$ have degree strictly more than $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ . Hence at most $2(\sqrt{\alpha}+4\cdot\varepsilon^{1/4})+4\varepsilon+32\beta)\cdot n\leq\sigma\cdot n$ vertices of $G$ have degree more than $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ (since $\varepsilon$ is small enough for this inequality to hold) and hence at most $\sigma\cdot n$ vertices of $G$ have degree more than $(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)n$ . Thus $G$ is as desired, a contradiction.
$\Diamond$
By Claim 10.9.4, we have that $\max\{e(G[A_{0}]),e(G[B_{0}])\}\geq\beta\cdot n^{2}$ . We assume without loss of generality that $e(G[A_{0}])\geq\beta\cdot n^{2}$ .
Claim 10.9.5.
There exists a negative edge $f_{1}=u_{1}v_{1}\in E(G^{\prime})$ with $|\phi(f_{1})|\geq(1-\alpha)|\phi(f_{0})|$ such that there exist at least $\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot n$ $u_{1}$ -obtuse vertices $w$ with $|\phi(wv_{1})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ .
Proof. Let $U:=\{w\in A_{0}:|N_{G}(w)\cap A_{0}|\geq\beta\cdot n\}$ . Since $e(G[A_{0}])\geq\beta\cdot n^{2}$ , it follows that $|U|\geq\beta\cdot n$ as otherwise $2\cdot e(G[A_{0}])=\sum_{v\in A_{0}}d_{G[A_{0}]}(v)\leq|U|\cdot n+\beta\cdot n\cdot|A_{0}|<2\beta\cdot n^{2}$ , a contradiction. By Claim 10.9.3(i) applied to $f_{0}$ , we have that $|(N(u)\cap N(v))\setminus\mathcal{N}_{0}|\leq(\sqrt{\alpha}+6\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})n$ .
Thus there exists $u_{1}\in U\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}$ since $\beta\geq 2\sqrt{\alpha}$ and $\varepsilon$ is small enough. Let
$U_{1}:=\bigg|\bigg\{w^{\prime}\in N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{1})\cap B_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}:|\phi(u_{1}w^{\prime})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg\}\bigg|$
By Claim 10.9.3(ii) applied to $f_{0}$ and $u_{1}\in A_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}$ , we have that
$|U_{1}|\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-10\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n>0,$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ is small enough. Thus there exists a vertex $v_{1}\in N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{1})\cap B_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}$ such that $|\phi(u_{1}v_{1})|\geq(1-\alpha)|\phi(f_{0})|$ . Let $f_{1}:=u_{1}v_{1}$ . Let
$V_{1}:=N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{1})\cap A_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg).$
By Claim 10.9.3(ii) applied to $f_{0}$ and $v_{1}\in B_{0}\cap\mathcal{N}_{0}$ , we have that
$|V_{1}|\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-10\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n>0,$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ is small enough. Since $V_{1}\subseteq A_{0}$ and $|A_{0}|=\frac{|N(u_{0})\cap N(v_{0})|}{2}\leq(\frac{1}{4}+\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ , we find that
$|A_{0}\setminus V_{1}|\leq(2\sqrt{\alpha}+11\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})n$
But now we calculate that
$|V_{1}\cap N_{G}(w)|\geq|N_{G}(w)\cap A_{0}|-|A_{0}\setminus V_{1}|\geq\beta\cdot n-\left(2\sqrt{\alpha}+11\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n\geq\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot n,$
where the last inequality follows since $\beta\geq 6\sqrt{\alpha}$ and $\varepsilon$ is small enough. Since $\phi$ is triangle-covered and $\alpha$ is small enough, it follows that for every $w^{\prime}\in V_{1}\cap N_{G}(w)$ , we have that $u_{1}w^{\prime}$ is a positive edge and hence $w^{\prime}$ is $u_{1}$ -obtuse with respect to $f_{1}$ . The claim follows.
$\Diamond$
Here is a useful claim that will help us to finish the proof.
Claim 10.9.6.
If $w\in V(G^{\prime})$ , then $\Delta(G[N_{G^{\prime}}(w)])\leq(\frac{1}{4}+\sqrt{\alpha})n$ and every vertex $w^{\prime}\in N_{G^{\prime}}(w)$ has degree in $G$ at most $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ .
Proof. Let $w^{\prime}\in N_{G^{\prime}}(w)$ . By definition of $G^{\prime}$ , we have that $ww^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}$ . Hence by Claim 10.9.2, we find that $|N(w)\cap N(w^{\prime})|\leq(\frac{1}{2}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ and $d_{G}(w^{\prime})\leq(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ and that $ww^{\prime}$ is $\alpha$ -balanced bipartite. By Proposition 10.8, we have that the number of vertices that are $w$ -obtuse with respect to $ww^{\prime}$ is at most
$\left(\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\alpha}\right)\cdot|N(u)\cap N(v)|\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+\sqrt{\alpha}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n\leq\left(\frac{1}{4}+\sqrt{\alpha}\right)n,$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ is small enough. Since $\phi$ is triangle-covered, we find for every $w^{\prime\prime}\in N_{G}(w^{\prime})\cap N_{G^{\prime}}(w)$ that $w^{\prime}w^{\prime\prime}$ is a positive edge and hence $w^{\prime\prime}$ is $w$ -obtuse with respect to $ww^{\prime}$ . The claim follows.
$\Diamond$
Let $f_{1}$ be as guaranteed by Claim 10.9.5. Since $\alpha\leq\frac{1}{4}$ as $\varepsilon$ is small enough, we find by Claim 10.9.2 that $|N(u_{1})\cap N(v_{1})|\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n$ and that $f_{1}$ is $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartite. Let $\mathcal{P}_{1}=(A_{1},B_{1})$ be an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition of $N(u_{1})\cap N(v_{1})$ . Let $\mathcal{N}_{1}$ denote the set of vertices of $N(u_{1})\cap N(v_{1})\cap V(G^{\prime})$ that are $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}_{1}$ and are not $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}$ -unclean with respect to $f_{1}$ .
Claim 10.9.7.
There exists $u_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}$ that is $u_{1}$ -obtuse with respect to $f_{1}$ such that $|\phi(u_{2}v_{1})|\geq(1-\alpha)|\phi(f_{0})|$ .
Proof. Since $\alpha$ is small enough, we have that $|\phi(f_{1})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\geq\frac{9}{10}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ . Hence by Claim 10.9.3(i), we find that $|(N(u_{1})\cap N(v_{1}))\setminus\mathcal{N}_{1}|\leq(\sqrt{\alpha}+6\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})\cdot n$ . By Claim 10.9.5, there exists at least $\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot n$ $u_{1}$ -obtuse vertices $w$ with $|\phi(wv_{1})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ . Since $\frac{\beta}{2}=3\sqrt{\alpha}\cdot n>(\sqrt{\alpha}+6\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})\cdot n$ as $\varepsilon$ is small enough, we find that there exists $u_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}$ that is $u_{1}$ -obtuse with respect to $f_{1}$ such that $|\phi(u_{2}v_{1})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ as desired.
$\Diamond$
Let $u_{2}$ be as guaranteed by Claim 10.9.7. We assume without loss of generality that $u_{2}\in A_{1}$ . Let $f_{2}:=u_{2}v_{1}$ . Since $\alpha\leq\frac{1}{4}$ as $\varepsilon$ is small enough, we find by Claim 10.9.2 that $|N(u_{2})\cap N(v_{1})|\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n$ and that $f_{2}$ is $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartite. Let $\mathcal{P}_{2}=(A_{2},B_{2})$ be an $\alpha$ -balanced-bipartition of $N(u_{2})\cap N(v_{1})$ . Let $\mathcal{N}_{2}$ denote the set of vertices of $N(u_{2})\cap N(v_{1})\cap V(G^{\prime})$ that are $\alpha$ -normal for $\mathcal{P}_{2}$ and are not $2\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}$ -unclean with respect to $f_{2}$ .
Since $\alpha$ is small enough, we have that $|\phi(u_{2}v_{1})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\geq\frac{9}{10}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ . Thus by Claim 10.9.3)(iii) applied to $f_{1}$ and $u_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}$ since $u_{1}\in V(G^{\prime})$ , we find that
$\bigg|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap(N(u_{1})\setminus N(v_{1}))\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-4\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)\bigg|\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)\cdot n,$
where we used that $(1-\alpha)\cdot(|\phi(f_{1})|+|\phi(u_{2}v_{1})|)-|\phi(f_{0})|\geq(1-\alpha)(2-2\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|-|\phi(f_{0})|\geq(1-4\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ .
Let $B:=B_{1}\cup(N(u_{1})\setminus N(v_{1}))$ and let $A:=V(G)\setminus B=A_{1}\cup(N(v_{1})\setminus N(u_{1}))$ . Since $u_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}$ , it follows from Claim 10.9.3(ii) that
$\bigg|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B_{1}\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-2\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)\bigg|\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-10\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n,$
where we used that $(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{1})|\geq(1-\alpha)^{2}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\geq(1-2\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ .
Combining these yields that
$\displaystyle\bigg|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})$
$\displaystyle\cap B\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-4\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)\bigg|$
$\displaystyle\geq\bigg|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B_{1}\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-2\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)\bigg|+\bigg|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap(N(u_{1})\setminus N(v_{1}))\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-4\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)\bigg|$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n.$
Since $u_{2}\in V(G^{\prime})$ as $u_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{1}$ , it follows by Claim 10.9.6 that
$\Delta(G[N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B])\leq\left(\frac{1}{4}+\sqrt{\alpha}\right)n$
and every vertex $w^{\prime}\in N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B$ has degree in $G$ at most $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ .
Note that
$|B|\leq|N(u_{1})\setminus N(v_{1})|+|B_{1}|\leq\frac{|N(u_{1})\cap N(v_{1})|}{2}+n-\delta(G)\leq\left(\frac{1}{4}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n+\left(\frac{1}{4}+\varepsilon\right)n\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n.$
Moreover
$\displaystyle e(G[A,B])$
$\displaystyle\geq|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B|\cdot\left(\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)n-|B\setminus N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})|-\Delta(G[N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B|)\right)$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n\cdot\left(\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)-\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{4}+\sqrt{\alpha}\right)\right)n$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2}-3\sqrt{\alpha}-21\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)\cdot n^{2}$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-5\sqrt{\alpha}-39\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)\cdot n^{2}$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-\sigma\right)\cdot n^{2},$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ is small enough.
Finally we proceed to show most vertices in $A$ have degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ as follows.
Claim 10.9.8.
There exists $v_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}$ that is $v_{1}$ -obtuse with respect to $f_{2}$ such that $|\phi(u_{2}v_{2})|\geq(1-4\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ .
Proof. Let $V_{2}:=N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B\cap\mathcal{H}\bigg((1-4\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\bigg)$ . Recall from before that
$|V_{2}|\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n.$ Since $d_{G}(v_{1})\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon)n$ , we find that
$|V_{2}\cap N_{G}(v_{1})|\geq|V_{2}|+d_{G}(v_{1})-n\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-19\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n.$
Note that since $\phi$ is triangle-covered, we have that every vertex of $V_{2}\cap N_{G}(v_{1})$ is in $N(u_{2})\cap N(v_{1})$ and is $v_{1}$ -obtuse. Since $\alpha$ is small enough, we have that $|\phi(f_{2})|\geq(1-\alpha)\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|\geq\frac{9}{10}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ . Hence by Claim 10.9.3(i), we find that $|(N(u_{2})\cap N(v_{1}))\setminus\mathcal{N}_{2}|\leq(\sqrt{\alpha}+6\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})\cdot n$ . Thus
$|V_{2}\cap N_{G}(v_{1})\cap\mathcal{N}_{2}|\geq|V_{2}\cap N_{G}(v_{1})|-|(N(u_{2})\cap N(v_{1}))\setminus\mathcal{N}_{2}|\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-3\sqrt{\alpha}-25\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n>0,$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ (and hence $\alpha$ ) is small enough. But then there exists $v_{2}\in V_{2}\cap N_{G}(v_{1})\cap\mathcal{N}_{2}$ as desired.
$\Diamond$
Let $v_{2}$ be as guaranteed by Claim 10.9.7. We assume without loss of generality that $v_{2}\in B_{2}$ . Since $\alpha$ is small enough, we have that $|\phi(u_{2}v_{2})|\geq\frac{9}{10}\cdot|\phi(f_{0})|$ . Thus by Claim 10.9.3(iii) applied to $f_{2}$ and $v_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}$ since $v_{1}\in V(G^{\prime})$ , we find that
$|N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})\cap(N(v_{1})\setminus N(u_{2}))|\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)\cdot n.$
Since $v_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}$ , it follows from Claim 10.9.3(ii) that
$|N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})\cap A_{2}|\geq\left(\frac{1}{4}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-10\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n.$
Since $A_{2}\subseteq N(u_{2})\cap N(v_{1})$ , we find that
$|N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})|\geq|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap A_{1}|+|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap(N(v_{1})\setminus N(u_{1}))|\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-28\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n.$
Recall that $E(G^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{H}\subseteq E^{-}(G)$ . Thus since $\phi$ is triangle-covered and $u_{2}v_{2}\in E^{-}(G)$ , we find that $N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})=\emptyset$ . Hence
$\displaystyle|N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})\cap B|$
$\displaystyle\leq|B|-|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B|$
$\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}\right)n-\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n$
$\displaystyle\leq(2\sqrt{\alpha}+20\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})n.$
Combining these yields that
$\displaystyle|N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})\cap A|$
$\displaystyle=|N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})|-|N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})\cap B|$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-28\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n-(2\sqrt{\alpha}+20\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6})n$
$\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-4\sqrt{\alpha}-48\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n$
Since $v_{2}\in V(G^{\prime})$ as $v_{2}\in\mathcal{N}_{2}$ , it follows by Claim 10.9.6 that every vertex $w^{\prime}\in N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})\cap A$ has degree in $G$ at most $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ . Since $(N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B)\cap(N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})\cap A)=\emptyset$ and every vertex of $N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B$ and every vertex of $N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})\cap A$ has degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+3\sqrt{\varepsilon})n$ (which is at most $(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)n$ ), we find that the number of vertices of $G$ with degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)n$ is at least
$|N_{G^{\prime}}(u_{2})\cap B|+|N_{G^{\prime}}(v_{2})\cap A|\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-2\sqrt{\alpha}-18\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n+\left(\frac{1}{2}-4\sqrt{\alpha}-48\cdot\varepsilon^{1/6}\right)n\geq(1-\sigma)n,$
where the last inequality follows since $\varepsilon$ (and hence $\alpha$ ) are small enough. Thus $G$ is as desired, a contradiction.
∎
We are now prepared to prove Theorem 1.6.
Proof of Theorem 1.6.
Follows from Theorem 10.9 and Lemma 2.2.
∎
11 A Robust Fractional Decomposition via Property Testing and Inheritance
In this section, we prove Theorem 2.6. First we recall our fractional stability theorem but to make our proof easier we make the following definition (which we also use extensively in Part III).
Definition 11.1.
Let $\sigma\geq 0$ . A graph $G$ on $n$ vertices is $\sigma$ -extremal if both of the following hold:
(i)
at least $(1-\sigma)n$ vertices of $G$ have degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)n$ , and
(ii)
the maximum cut of $G$ is at least $(1-\sigma)\cdot\frac{n^{2}}{4}$ .
With this definition, we may restate our fractional stability theorem as follows.
Theorem 11.2 (Fractional Stability Restated).
For each sufficiently small $\sigma>0$ , there exists $\varepsilon>0$ such that the following holds for all sufficiently large $n$ : If $G$ is a $K_{3}$ -divisible graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon\right)n$ and $G$ has no fractional $K_{3}$ -decomposition, then $G$ is $\sigma$ -extremal.
For our proof, we need a result from property testing. To that end, we recall the following definition from the literature.
Definition 11.3.
A graph parameter $\gamma$ (i.e. a function $\gamma$ for all graphs $G$ to real numbers) is estimable if for all reals $\varepsilon,\rho>0$ there exists $k_{0}>0$ such that for all $n\geq k\geq k_{0}$ , we have that if $G$ is a graph on $n$ vertices and $G[k]$ is an induced subgraph of $G$ on $k$ vertices chosen uniformly at random, then
$\mathbb{P}\left[|\gamma(G[k])-\gamma(G)|>\varepsilon\right]\leq\rho.$
The key fact that we need is the following but first a definition. For a graph $G$ , we let $\textrm{MaxCut}(G)$ denote the number of edges in a maximum cut of $G$ . We let $t_{\textrm{cut}}(G):=\frac{\textrm{MaxCut}(G)}{v(G)^{2}}$ .
Theorem 11.4.
$t_{\textrm{cut}}$ is estimable.
Theorem 11.4 was originally established in some of the earliest papers on property testing, namely by Arora, Karger, and Karpinski [2] from 1995 and independently Goldreich, Goldwasser, and Ron [32] from 1996 (see [3] and [33] for the journal versions of both articles respectively). Since then, general characterizations of estimable parameters have been established from which it is fairly straightforward to see that max cut is estimable (indeed, it is used as one of the canonical parameters having this property). See Example 15.2 in the book by Lovász [47] on graph limits for one of these more straightforward derivations.
We also need a lemma regarding degrees in a sampled subgraph as follows.
Lemma 11.5.
For all reals $\varepsilon,\rho>0$ , there exists an integer $k_{0}>0$ such that the following holds for all integers $n\geq k\geq k_{0}$ : if $G$ is a graph on $n$ vertices and $S$ is a subset of $V(G)$ of size $k$ chosen uniformly at random, then for each $e\in E(G)$ we have that
$\mathbb{P}\left[\exists~v\in S\textrm{ such that }\frac{d_{G[S]}(v)}{k}<\frac{d_{G}(v)}{n}-\varepsilon~\bigg|~e\subseteq S\right]<\rho.$
Proof.
Follows from the Chernoff bounds and the union bound.
∎
Now we use Theorem 11.4 and Lemma 11.5 to prove the following lemma.
Lemma 11.6.
For all reals $\varepsilon,\rho,\sigma>0$ , there exists an integer $k_{0}>0$ such that the following holds for all integers $n\geq k\geq k_{0}$ : Let $G$ be a graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon)n$ that is not $\sigma$ -extremal. If $S$ is a subset of $V(G)$ of size $k$ chosen uniformly at random, then for each $e\in E(G)$ we have that
$\mathbb{P}\left[\delta(G[S])\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-2\varepsilon\right)k\text{ and $G[S]$ is not $\sigma/2$-extremal}~\bigg|~e\subseteq S\right]\geq 1-\rho.$
Proof.
We choose $k_{0}$ large enough as needed throughout the proof. By Lemma 11.5 since $k_{0}$ is large enough and $\delta(G)\geq(\frac{3}{4}-\varepsilon)n$ , we find that
$\mathbb{P}\left[\delta(G[S])<\left(\frac{3}{4}-2\varepsilon\right)k~\bigg|~e\subseteq S\right]<\frac{\rho}{2}.$
Since $G$ is not $\sigma$ -extremal, $G$ violates condition (i) or condition (ii) of the definition.
First suppose $G$ violates condition (i), that is, there are fewer than $(1-\sigma)n$ vertices of $G$ with degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)$ n or equivalently there are strictly more than $\sigma n$ vertices of $G$ with degree more than $(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)n$ . Let $B:=\{v\in V(G):d_{G}(v)>(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)n\}$ . Thus $|B|>\sigma n$ . By Lemma 11.5 since $k_{0}$ is large enough, we find that
$\mathbb{P}\left[G[S]\text{ is $\sigma/2$-extremal}~\bigg|~e\subset S\right]\leq\mathbb{P}\left[\exists v\in B\text{ such that }\frac{d_{G[S]}}{k}<\frac{d_{G}(v)}{n}-\frac{\sigma}{2}~\bigg|~e\subset S\right]<\frac{\rho}{2}.$
Combining the two above inequalities then yields the desired result.
So we assume $G$ violates condition (ii), that is, $\textrm{MaxCut}(G)<(1-\sigma)\cdot\frac{n^{2}}{4}$ and hence $t_{\textrm{cut}}(G)<\frac{1-\sigma}{4}$ . Let $u,v\in V(G)$ such that $e=uv$ . Let $G^{\prime}:=G\setminus\{u,v\}$ . Let $S^{\prime}$ be a subset of $V(G^{\prime})$ of size $k-2$ chosen uniformly at random. Note that $S^{\prime}\cup\{u,v\}$ has the same probability distribution as $S$ conditioned on the even $e\subseteq S$ . Since $n$ is large enough, it follows that $t_{\textrm{cut}}(G^{\prime})<\frac{1-0.9\sigma}{4}$ . By Theorem 11.4, we find that
$\mathbb{P}\left[t_{\textrm{cut}}(G^{\prime}[S^{\prime}])<\frac{1-0.7\sigma}{4}\right]<\frac{\rho}{2}.$
Note that if $t_{\textrm{cut}}(G^{\prime}[S^{\prime}])<\frac{1-0.7\sigma}{4}$ , then since $n$ is large enough, we have that $t_{\textrm{cut}}(G[S^{\prime}\cup\{u,v\}])<\frac{1-0.5\sigma}{4}$ . Thus $\mathbb{P}\left[G[S]\text{ is $\sigma/2$-extremal}~\bigg|~e\subset S\right]<\frac{\rho}{2}$ and combining this with our first inequality yields the desired result.
∎
Lemma 11.6 would be enough when combined with Theorem 1.6 to show that if $G$ is a graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ , then there exists a $C$ -low-weight $\rho$ -almost fractional triangle decomposition of $G$ (for some fixed integer $C\geq 1$ ). To show that we can also obtain such a fractional packing that is $\sigma$ -seeded (for some $\sigma\in(0,1]$ ) and furthermore to show that the packing can be further corrected to a fractional triangle decomposition, we require the following lemma which employs ideas from our paper [13] which in turn was inspired by ideas of Montgomery in [48].
First let us recall some extra terminology about fractional packings as follows.
Definition 11.7.
Let $F$ and $G$ be graphs. If $\phi$ is a fractional $F$ -packing of $G$ and $e\in E(G)$ , we write $\partial\phi(e):=\sum_{H\subseteq G:e\in E(H)}\phi(H)$ .
Here is a useful proposition about achieving any target fractional packing for a subset $R$ of $E(G)$ in a graph $G$ provided that $G$ admits a fractional decomposition for $G-R^{\prime}$ for any $R^{\prime}\subseteq R$ .
Proposition 11.8.
Let $F$ and $G$ be graphs. If $R\subseteq E(G)$ such that for each $R^{\prime}\subseteq R$ we have that $G-R^{\prime}$ admits a fractional $F$ -decomposition, then for any $\varphi:E(R)\rightarrow[0,1]$ there exists a fractional $F$ -packing of $G$ with $\partial\phi(e)=1$ for $e\in E(G)\setminus R$ and $\partial\phi(e)=\varphi(e)$ for $e\in R.$
Proof.
Let $k:=|R|$ and let $e_{1},\ldots e_{k}$ be an enumeration of $R$ such that $\varphi(e_{1})\leq\varphi(e_{2})\leq\ldots\leq\varphi(e_{k})$ . Let $R_{0}:=\emptyset$ and for each $i\in[k]$ , let $R_{i}:=\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{i}\}$ . Let $w_{0}:=\varphi(e_{1})$ , $w_{k}:=1-\varphi(e_{k})$ and for each $i\in[k-1]$ , let $w_{i}:=\varphi(e_{i+1})-\varphi(e_{i})$ . By assumption for each $i\in\{0,1,\ldots,k\}$ , we have that $G-R_{i}$ admits a fractional $F$ -decomposition $\phi_{i}$ . We define $\phi:=\sum_{i=0}^{k}w_{i}\cdot\phi_{i}$ . Thus $\phi$ is a fractional $F$ -packing of $G$ such that for each $e\in E(G)\setminus R$ , we have that $\partial\phi(e)=\sum_{i=0}^{k}w_{i}=1$ . On the other hand, we have for each $i\in[k]$ that $\phi(e_{i})=\sum_{j=0}^{i}w_{i}=\varphi(e_{i})$ . Thus $\phi$ is as desired.
∎
We now use the above proposition to prove the following lemma which asserts that if when deleting low degree subgraphs of a graph $G$ we still admit fractional decompositions, then $G$ admits any fractional packing of target weights provided they are close enough to $1$ .
Lemma 11.9.
Let $F$ be a graph and let $\varepsilon\in(0,1]$ be real. Let $G$ be a graph on $n$ vertices such that for all $H\subseteq G$ with $\Delta(H)\leq\lceil 2\varepsilon n\rceil$ , we have that $G-E(H)$ admits a fractional $F$ -decomposition. If $\varphi:E(G)\rightarrow[1-\varepsilon,1]$ , then there exists a fractional $F$ -packing $\phi$ of $G$ such that $\partial\phi(e)=\varphi(e)$ for all $e\in E(G)$ .
Proof.
Since $\Delta(G)\leq n-1$ , we have by Vizing’s Theorem that there exist an edge-coloring of $G$ with at most $\Delta(G)+1\leq n$ colors. Let $d:=\lceil 2\varepsilon n\rceil$ and $C:=\lfloor\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\rfloor$ . Note that $C\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}$ and hence $\varepsilon C\leq 1$ . Furthermore, $n\leq Cd$ since $2\varepsilon\cdot\lfloor 1/\varepsilon\rfloor\geq 1$ for all $\varepsilon\in(0,1]$ . By taking the unions of $d$ color classes, we find that there exists a partition $R_{1},\ldots,R_{C}$ of $E(G)$ such that $\Delta(R_{i})\leq d$ for each $i\in[C]$ . By our assumption on $G$ , we have for each $i\in[C]$ and $R^{\prime}\subseteq R_{i}$ that $G-R^{\prime}$ admits a fractional $F$ -decomposition. For each $i\in[C]$ , let $\varphi_{i}(e):=1$ for $e\in E(G)\setminus R_{i}$ and let $\varphi_{i}(e):=1-C\cdot(1-\varphi(e))$ . Note that since $\varphi:E(G)\rightarrow[1-\varepsilon,1]$ , we find that $\varphi_{i}:E(G)\rightarrow[1-\varepsilon C,1]$ for each $i\in[C]$ . Since $\varepsilon C\leq 1$ , we further find that $\varphi_{i}:E(G)\rightarrow[1-\varepsilon C,1]$ for each $i\in[C]$ .
Thus by Proposition 11.8, we have for each $i\in[k]$ that there exists a fractional $F$ -packing $\phi_{i}$ of $G$ such that $\partial\phi_{i}(e)=1$ for $e\in E(G)\setminus R_{i}$ and $\partial\phi_{i}(e)=\varphi_{i}(e)$ for $e\in R_{i}$ . Now let $\phi:=\frac{1}{C}\cdot\sum_{i\in[C]}\phi_{i}$ . Since $\phi_{i}$ is a fractional $F$ -packing of $G$ for each $i\in[C]$ , it follows that $\phi$ is a fractional $F$ -packing of $G$ . Furthermore, for each $e\in E(G)$ , we find that
$\partial\phi(e)=\frac{1}{C}\cdot\sum_{i\in C}\partial\phi_{i}(e)=1+\frac{1}{C}\cdot(-C(1-\varphi(e))=\varphi(e)$
as desired.
∎
We are now prepared to prove a general lemma which asserts that if a graph $G$ has a family of subgraph $\mathcal{H}$ where every edge is in roughly the same number of elements of $\mathcal{H}$ and each graph in $\mathcal{H}$ admits a fractional $F$ -decomposition under deletion of small maximum degree subgraphs, then $G$ admits a $C$ -regular fractional $F$ -decomposition for some integer $C\geq 1$ .
First we introduce some more notation. If $\mathcal{H}$ is a family of subgraphs of a graph $G$ and $e\in E(G)$ , then we let $\mathcal{H}(e):=\{H\in\mathcal{H}:e\in E(H)\}$ .
Lemma 11.10.
Let $F$ be a graph and let $C\geq 1$ and let $\varepsilon:=\frac{1}{2C}$ . If $G$ is a graph and $\mathcal{H}$ is a family of subgraphs of $G$ such that all of the following hold:
(a)
$v(H)=k$ for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ ,
(b)
for each $e\in E(G)$ ,
$\frac{1-\varepsilon}{C}\cdot n^{k-2}\leq|\mathcal{H}(e)|\leq\frac{1}{C}\cdot n^{k-2},$
(c)
for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ and $H_{0}\subseteq H$ with $\Delta(H_{0})\leq\lceil 4\varepsilon k\rceil$ , we have that $H-E(H_{0})$ admits a fractional $F$ -decomposition,
then $G$ has a $2C$ -regular fractional $F$ -decomposition.
Proof.
Let $\mathcal{F}$ be the set of copies of $F$ in $G$ . In order to seed the fractional decomposition, we first define a fractional $F_{0}$ -packing as follows. For each $T\in\mathcal{F}$ , we let $\phi_{0}(T):=w_{0}:=\frac{\varepsilon}{n^{V(F)-2}}$ . Let $h_{0}:=\frac{1-\varepsilon}{C}\cdot n^{k-2}$ . For each $e\in E(G)$ , we let
$\varphi(e):=(1-w_{0}\cdot|\mathcal{F}(e)|)\cdot\frac{h_{0}}{|\mathcal{H}(e)|}.$
By condition (b), we find that
$1\geq\frac{h_{0}}{|\mathcal{H}(e)|}\geq 1-\varepsilon.$
Since $|\mathcal{F}(e)|\leq n^{V(F)-2}$ , we also find that $w_{0}\cdot|\mathcal{F}(e)|\leq\varepsilon$ . Since $(1-\varepsilon)^{2}\geq 1-2\varepsilon$ , we find that
$\varphi(e)\in[1-2\varepsilon,1].$
Thus we find that $\varphi:E(G)\rightarrow[1-2\varepsilon,1]$ . Note $\varepsilon=\frac{1}{2C}\leq\frac{1}{2}$ and hence we also have that $\varphi:E(G)\rightarrow[0,1]$ . Now let $\varepsilon^{\prime}:=2\varepsilon$ . By (c), we have for all $H\in\mathcal{H}$ and $H_{0}\subseteq H$ with $\Delta(H_{0})\leq\lceil 2\varepsilon^{\prime}k\rceil$ that $H-H_{0}$ admits a fractional $F$ -decomposition. Hence by Lemma 11.9, there exists a fractional $F$ -packing $\phi_{H}$ of $G$ such that $\partial\phi_{H}(e)=\varphi(e)$ for all $e\in E(G)$ . Let
$\phi:=\phi_{0}+\frac{1}{h_{0}}\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\phi_{H}.$
Thus for each $e\in E(G)$ we find that
$\displaystyle\partial\phi(e)$
$\displaystyle=\partial\phi_{0}(e)+\frac{1}{h_{0}}\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\partial\phi_{H}(e)=w_{0}\cdot|\mathcal{F}(e)|+\frac{1}{h_{0}}\cdot\varphi(e)\cdot|\mathcal{H}(e)|$
$\displaystyle=w_{0}\cdot|\mathcal{F}(e)|+(1-w_{0}\cdot|\mathcal{F}(e)|)=1$
and hence $\phi$ is a fractional $F$ -decomposition of $G$ . Note that for each $T\in\mathcal{F}$ , we have that $\phi(T)\geq\phi_{0}(T)\geq w_{0}$ . Thus $\phi$ is $\frac{1}{2C}$ -seeded. On the other hand for each $T\in\mathcal{F}$ , we have that $|\{H\in\mathcal{H}:T\subseteq H\}|\leq n^{k-v(F)}$ and hence we calculate that
$\displaystyle\phi(T)$
$\displaystyle=\phi_{0}(T)+\frac{1}{h_{0}}\cdot\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}\phi_{H}(T)\leq w_{0}+\frac{C}{(1-\varepsilon)\cdot n^{V(F)-2}}$
$\displaystyle\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{n^{v(F)-2}}+\frac{C(1+\varepsilon)}{n^{v(F)-2}}=\frac{1}{n^{v(F)-2}}\cdot\left(\frac{1}{2C}+C+\frac{1}{2}\right)$
$\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n^{v(F)-2}}\cdot(C+1)\leq\frac{2C}{n^{v(F)-2}},$
and hence $\phi$ is $2C$ -low-weight. Altogether then, we find that $\phi$ is a $C$ -regular fractional $F$ -decomposition of $G$ as desired.
∎
We are now prepared to prove Theorem 2.6.
Proof of Theorem 2.6.
We choose $\varepsilon$ small enough as needed throughout the proof. Let $\varepsilon^{\prime}:=7\varepsilon$ and let $\sigma^{\prime}:=\frac{\sigma}{2}$ . We crucially note that since $\varepsilon$ is small enough, we have that $\varepsilon^{\prime}$ is small enough to satisfy Theorem 1.6 for $\sigma^{\prime}$ .
Suppose for a contradiction that $G$ is not $\sigma$ -extremal. Set $\rho:=\varepsilon$ . Let $k_{0}$ be as in Lemma 11.6 for $\varepsilon,\rho,\sigma$ . We choose $k\geq k_{0}$ such that $\lceil 4\varepsilon k\rceil\leq 5\varepsilon k$ . Let
$\mathcal{H}:=\bigg\{G[S]:S\subseteq V(G),~|S|=k,~\delta(G[S])\geq\left(\frac{3}{4}-2\varepsilon\right)k\text{ and $G[S]$ is not $\sigma/2$-extremal}\bigg\}.$
By Lemma 11.6, we find for each $e\in E(G)$ that
$(1-\rho)\cdot\binom{n-2}{k-2}\leq|\mathcal{H}(e)|\leq\binom{n-2}{k-2}.$
Let $C:=\frac{1}{2}\cdot n^{k-2}/\binom{n-2}{k-2}$ . Let $F:=K_{3}$ . Thus $G$ and $\mathcal{H}$ satisfy Lemma 11.10(a) and (b) for $F$ , $\varepsilon$ and $C/2$ .
Further note that for each $H\in\mathcal{H}$ and $H_{0}\subseteq H$ with $\Delta(H_{0})\leq\lceil 4\varepsilon k\rceil$ , we have that $\Delta(H-E(H_{0}))\geq(\frac{3}{4}-7\varepsilon)k$ and hence $H-E(H_{0})$ admits a triangle decomposition by Theorem 1.6 as $H$ is not $\sigma^{\prime}$ -extremal. Thus $G$ and $\mathcal{H}$ satisfy Lemma 11.10(c) for $F$ , $\varepsilon$ and $C/2$ .
By Lemma 11.10, it follows that $G$ has a $C$ -regular fractional $K_{3}$ -decomposition, a contradiction.
∎
Part III Proof of Nash-Williams’ Conjecture
12 Proof Overview
Before breaking our proof of Nash-Williams’ Conjecture into cases, we need the following notation and recall the definition of extremal for Nash-Williams’ Conjecture.
Definition 12.1.
We write $T_{2}(n):=K_{\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor,\lceil\frac{n}{2}\rceil}$ for the Turán graph $T(n,2)$ .
Definition 12.2.
Let $\sigma\geq 0$ . A graph $G$ on $n$ vertices is $\sigma$ -extremal if both of the following hold:
(i)
at least $(1-\sigma)n$ vertices of $G$ have degree at most $(\frac{3}{4}+\sigma)n$ , and
(ii)
the maximum cut of $G$ is at least $(1-\sigma)\cdot\frac{n^{2}}{4}$ .
We recall from the literature that graphs $G$ and $H$ on the same number of vertices are $\alpha$ -close if one can add and/or delete at most $\alpha\cdot v(G)^{2}$ edges to transform $G$ into a graph isomorphic to $H$ . We also recall that the join of graphs $H_{1}$ and $H_{2}$ , denoted $H_{1}+H_{2}$ , is the graph obtained by taking the vertex-disjoint union of $H_{1}$ and $H_{2}$ and adding a complete bipartite graph between $V(H_{1})$ and $V(H_{2})$ . Also recall that $\overline{G}$ denotes the complement of a graph $G$ .
Recall our strategy is to divide the proof of Nash-Williams’ Conjecture into five cases as follows: $G$ is
(1)
not $\beta$ -extremal
(2)
$\beta$ -extremal but not in (3)-(5)
(3)
$\alpha$ -close to $\overline{T(n/2)}+\overline{T(n/2)}$
(4)
$\alpha$ -close to $\overline{T(n/2)}+T(n/2)$
(5)
$\alpha$ -close to $T(n/2)+T(n/2)$ .
Remark 12.3.
Note in the cases above we should write $\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor$ for one graph and $\lceil\frac{n}{2}\rceil$ for the other as $n$ might not be even. However, for the sake of the reader we omit the floors and ceilings since in the actual proofs we will fix a partition that is $\alpha$ -close to a balanced complete blow-up of the associated family of partitioned triangles (see Definition 15.13) rather than an edit-distance close mapping to a specific graph.
Here are the corresponding theorems for the five cases.
Theorem 12.4.
There exists $\beta_{0}>0$ such that for every real $\beta\in(0,\beta_{0}]$ , there exists an integer $n_{0}$ such that for all $n\geq n_{0}$ the following holds: If $G$ is a $K_{3}$ -divisible graph on $n\geq n_{0}$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ and $G$ is not $\beta$ -extremal, then $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition.
Theorem 12.5.
For every real $\alpha>0$ , there exists a real $\beta>0$ and an integer $n_{0}$ such that for all $n\geq n_{0}$ the following holds: If $G$ is a $K_{3}$ -divisible graph on $n\geq n_{0}$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ and $G$ is $\beta$ -extremal but $G$ is not $\alpha$ -close to any of $T_{2}(n/2)+T_{2}(n/2)$ , $\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}+T_{2}(n/2)$ , or $\overline{T_{2}(n/2})+\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}$ , then $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition.
Theorem 12.6.
There exists a real $\alpha>0$ and an integer $n_{0}$ such that for all $n\geq n_{0}$ the following holds: If $G$ is a $K_{3}$ -divisible graph on $n\geq n_{0}$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ and $G$ is $\alpha$ -close to $\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}+\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}$ , then $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition.
Theorem 12.7.
There exists a real $\alpha>0$ and an integer $n_{0}$ such that for all $n\geq n_{0}$ the following holds: If $G$ is a $K_{3}$ -divisible graph on $n\geq n_{0}$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ and $G$ is $\alpha$ -close to $\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}+T_{2}(n/2)$ , then $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition.
Theorem 12.8.
There exists a real $\alpha>0$ and an integer $n_{0}$ such that for all $n\geq n_{0}$ the following holds: If $G$ is a $K_{3}$ -divisible graph on $n\geq n_{0}$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ and $G$ is $\alpha$ -close to $T_{2}(n/2)+T_{2}(n/2)$ , then $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition.
12.1 Outline of Part III
In Section 13, we introduce partitioned graphs. Section 13.1 contains the requisite definitions. Section 13.2 states our main decomposition theorem which is a special case of the main ‘black-box’ decomposition theorem from [17]. Section 13.3 characterizes the fractional and integral divisibility conditions for our four special cases.
In Section 14, we prove our main absorber stability lemma and idealized decomposition theorems. Namely, in Section 14.1, we prove our absorber stability lemma, Lemma 14.4. In Section 14.3, we prove our various idealized decomposition theorems for the different cases.
In Section 15, we prove the theorems of the various cases. In Section 15.2, we prove a few cleaning lemmas that show how to clean a graph to the divisibility conditions for our cases so as to apply the corresponding idealized decomposition theorems. Then we proceed in the remainder of the section to prove our various cases of Nash-Williams’ Conjectures, with the cases for $\mathcal{F}_{1}$ and $\mathcal{F}_{2}$ requiring parity fixers (cases (3) and (4) above) and the case of $\mathcal{F}_{3}$ (case (5) above) requiring more careful attention due to its stringent divisibility conditions.
12.2 Proof of Nash-Williams’ Conjecture
Assuming the theorems above, we are now prepared to prove Theorem 1.8, that is Nash-Williams’ Conjecture.
Proof of Nash-Williams’ Conjecture.
We choose $n$ sufficiently large as needed throughout the proof. Let $\alpha_{1}$ be as in Theorem 12.6, $\alpha_{2}$ be as in Theorem 12.7, and $\alpha_{3}$ be as in Theorem 12.8. Let $\alpha:=\min\{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\}$ . Let $\beta$ be as in Theorem 12.5 for $\alpha$ . Let $\beta_{0}$ be as in Theorem 12.4 and let $\beta^{\prime}:=\min\{\beta_{0},\beta\}$ . Recall that $G$ is a $K_{3}$ -divisible graph on $n$ vertices with $\delta(G)\geq\frac{3}{4}n$ .
First suppose that $G$ is $\alpha$ -close to $T_{2}(n/2)+T_{2}(n/2)$ . Then by Theorem 12.8, $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition as desired. So we assume $G$ is not $\alpha$ -close to $T_{2}(n/2)+T_{2}(n/2)$ .
Next suppose that $G$ is $\alpha$ -close to $\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}+T_{2}(n/2)$ . Then by Theorem 12.7, $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition as desired. So we assume $G$ is not $\alpha$ -close to $\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}+T_{2}(n/2)$ .
Next suppose that $G$ is $\alpha$ -close to $\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}+\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}$ . Then by Theorem 12.6, $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition as desired. So we assume $G$ is not $\alpha$ -close to $\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}+\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}$ .
Next suppose that $G$ is $\beta^{\prime}$ -extremal. Since $\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta$ , we find that $G$ is also $\beta$ -extremal. Since $G$ is not $\alpha$ -close to any of $T_{2}(n/2)+T_{2}(n/2)$ , $\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}+T_{2}(n/2)$ , or $\overline{T_{2}(n/2})+\overline{T_{2}(n/2)}$ , we have by Theorem 12.5 that $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition as desired.
Hence we assume that $G$ is not $\beta^{\prime}$ -extremal. Since $\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta_{0}$ and $n$ is large enough, we have by Theorem 12.4 that $G$ has a $K_{3}$ -decomposition as desired.
∎
13 Partitioned Graphs, Divisibility and a Decomposition Theorem
In this section, we proceed to provide the many definitions we need about partitioned graphs, state our ‘black-box’ partitioned decomposition theorem which is a special case of the main decomposition theorem from [17], and then characterize integral and fractional divisibility for our various families of partitioned triangles.
13.1 Partitioned Graphs and Divisibility
Definition 13.1 (Partitioned Graph).
Let $t\geq 1$ be an integer. A $t$ -partitioned graph is a graph $G$ together with a partition $\mathcal{P}_{G}=(V_{1},\ldots,V_{t})$ of $V(G)$ . We use $e_{ij}(G)$ for $i\neq j\in[t]$ to denote $e(G[V_{i},V_{j}])$ and $e_{ii}(G)$ to denote $e(G[V_{i}])$ ; we let $\vec{e}(G)$ denote the vector $(e_{ij}(G):i\leq j\in[t])$ . For $v\in V(G)$ and $i\in[t]$ , we let $d_{i}(v):=|N_{G}(v)\cap V_{i}|$ and let $\vec{d}_{G}(v)$ denote the vector $(d_{i}(v):i\in[t])$ .
Definition 13.2 ( $\mathcal{F}$ -decomposition).
Let $t\geq 1$ be integers. Let $F$ and $G$ be $t$ -partitioned graphs. A copy of $F$ in $G$ , or $F$ -copy for short, is a subgraph $H$ of $G$ such that there exists an isomorphism $\phi$ from $H$ to $F$ such that $\mathcal{P}_{F}(\phi(v))=\mathcal{P}_{G}(v)$ for all $v\in V(H)$ for all $e\in H$ and $v\in V(e)$ . An $F$ -decomposition of $G$ is a partition of the edges of $G$ into copies of $F$ . Let $\mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{k}\}$ be a family of $t$ -partitioned graphs. An $\mathcal{F}$ -copy of $G$ is an $F_{i}$ -copy of $G$ for some $i\in[k]$ . An $\mathcal{F}$ -decomposition of $G$ is a partition of the edges of $G$ into $\mathcal{F}$ -copies.
Definition 13.3 ( $\mathcal{F}$ -blowups).
Let $t\geq 1$ be integers. Let $F$ and $G$ be $t$ -partitioned graphs. We say $G$ is an $F$ -blowup if for all $i\leq j\in[t]$ , we have that $e_{ij}(G)\neq 0$ if and only if $e_{ij}(F)\neq 0$ . Let $\mathcal{F}=\{F_{1},\ldots,F_{k}\}$ be a family of $t$ -partitioned graphs. We say $G$ is an $\mathcal{F}$ -blowup if for all $i\leq j\in[t]$ , we have that $e_{ij}(G)\neq 0$ if there exists $\ell\in[k]$ such that $e_{ij}(F_{\ell})\neq 0$ .
To characterize the necessary divisibility conditions for an $\mathcal{F}$ -decomposition (what is called $\mathcal{F}$ -divisible) and similarly for integral $\mathcal{F}$ -decompositions (what is called integrally $\mathcal{F}$ -divisible) and fractional $\mathcal{F}$ -decompositions (what is called fractionally $\mathcal{F}$ -divisible), we require the following standard concepts from linear algebra, convex geometry, and optimization.
Definition 13.4.
